    The **Robust Scaler** modifies the dataset by removing the median and scaling the data based on a predetermined quantile range, regularly the interquartile range (IQR), which spans the first and third quartiles. This method is similar to the Standard Scaler, except instead of using the mean and standard deviation, it utilizes the median and quantiles. This distinction makes it more resistant to the effects of outliers.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-Robust_Scaler_19Aug2024.png)
    
    Robust Scaler is especially suited for this scenario since the **CreditScore** feature has significant outliers (values as low as **5**), and using a method sensitive to outliers could adversely affect model performance.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Robust Scaler.**
    
    The option that says: **Standard Scaler** is incorrect because it simply standardizes features by removing the mean and scaling to unit variance, which may not handle outliers effectively, as outliers can significantly affect the mean and variance.
    
    The option that says: **L2 Normalization** is incorrect because this option primarily transforms data based on mean and standard deviation, which can be influenced by outliers and is not ideal for datasets with extreme outliers.
    
    The option that says: **Z-score normalization** is incorrect because it is typically used to scale feature vectors to have a unit norm, but it doesn’t address the issue of outliers in the dataset effectively.
    
- Standard Scaler

A machine learning (ML) team is working on a project that involves training and deploying machine learning models.

Select the correct AWS service from the following list for each task. Each AWS service should be selected one or more times. (Select THREE.)

- Automate the entire process of training, testing, and deploying the models.
    - **AWS CodePipeline**
    - AWS CodeBuild
    - AWS CodeDeploy
- Compile the code, execute unit tests, and build the necessary artifacts.
    - AWS CodeDeploy
    - AWS CodePipeline
    - **AWS CodeBuild**
- Automatically release new versions of the models to various environments while avoiding downtime and handling the complexity of updating them.
    - AWS CodePipeline
    - AWS CodeBuild
    - **AWS CodeDeploy**
- **Evaluation**
    
    **AWS CodeBuild** is a fully managed continuous integration service that compiles source code, runs tests, and produces software packages that are ready for deployment. It eliminates the need to provision, manage, and scale your own build servers. With CodeBuild, you can define your build specifications as a series of build commands in a buildspec file.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-codebuild-19-Aug-2024.png)
    
    **AWS CodePipeline** is a fully managed continuous delivery service that helps you automate your release pipelines for fast and reliable application and infrastructure updates. CodePipeline automates the build, test, and deploy phases of your release process every time there is a code change, based on the release model you define.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-codepipeline-19-Aug-2024.png)
    
    **AWS CodeDeploy** is a fully managed deployment service that automates software deployments to a variety of compute services such as Amazon EC2, AWS Fargate, AWS Lambda, and your on-premises servers. CodeDeploy makes it easier to rapidly release new features, helps avoid downtime during application deployment, and handles the complexity of updating your applications.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-codedeploy-19-Aug-2024-b.png)
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    – Automate the entire process of training, testing, and deploying the models: **AWS CodePipeline**
    
    – Compile the code, execute unit tests, and build the necessary artifacts: **AWS CodeBuild**
    
    – Automatically release new versions of the models to various environments while avoiding downtime and handling the complexity of updating them: **AWS CodeDeploy**
    

A team is working on a machine learning project that involves training a recommendation model using the Amazon SageMaker seq2seq algorithm. The dataset is stored in Amazon S3. The goal is to convert this training dataset into the RecordIO-Protobuf format, which is required for efficient training with SageMaker Pipe mode.

Which of the following options would be most suitable for this use case?

- **Set up an Amazon EMR cluster, load the dataset from S3, and leverage Apache Spark to perform the necessary conversions.**
    
    **Apache Spark** is a distributed processing framework and programming model that facilitates machine learning, stream processing, and graph analytics with Amazon EMR clusters. Like Apache Hadoop, Spark is an open-source, distributed processing system commonly utilized for big data workloads. However, Spark differs from Hadoop MapReduce in several significant ways. Spark features an optimized directed acyclic graph (DAG) execution engine and actively caches data in memory, enhancing performance, especially for specific algorithms and interactive queries.
    
    ![Amazon EMR - Apache Spark](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/amazon-emr-081924.png)
    
    **Amazon EMR (Elastic MapReduce)** is a managed big data platform that allows you to process large datasets using popular frameworks like Apache Spark.
    
    **EMR Cluster**: EMR provides a scalable and cost-effective way to process data. You can create an EMR cluster, which automatically provisions the necessary resources (such as EC2 instances) for your Spark jobs.
    
    Spark is a powerful distributed data processing framework. You can write Spark jobs to read the dataset from Amazon S3, transform it into the RecordIO-Protobuf format, and save it back to S3. Spark’s parallel processing capabilities make it efficient for large-scale data conversion.
    
    **RecordIO-Protobuf Format**: EMR allows you to install additional libraries (such as protobuf) on the cluster, enabling you to serialize your data into the required format.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Set up an Amazon EMR cluster, load the dataset from S3, and leverage Apache Spark to perform the necessary conversions.**
    
    The option that says: **Deploy an AWS Fargate task that runs a custom containerized application that is responsible for converting the dataset** is incorrect. ****While Fargate is suitable for running containerized applications, it’s not the most straightforward solution for this specific use case. Setting up a custom containerized application introduces unnecessary complexity. You’d need to manage the container, orchestration, and data transfer.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Data Firehose to ingest the dataset from S3 and transform it into RecordIO-Protobuf format** is incorrect because Data Firehose is primarily designed for real-time data streaming, not batch processing. Additionally, Data Firehose is optimized for continuous data ingestion and transformation. It’s not ideal for converting an entire dataset in a batch fashion.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize Amazon Redshift Spectrum to query the dataset stored in S3 and perform the necessary conversions** is incorrect. Redshift Spectrum is a query service for analyzing data in S3 using SQL queries. However, it’s not meant for format conversion. Redshift Spectrum is only used for analytical queries rather than data transformation.
    
- Deploy an AWS Fargate task that runs a custom containerized application that is responsible for converting the dataset.
- Utilize Amazon Redshift Spectrum to query the dataset stored in S3 and perform the necessary conversions.
- Use Amazon Data Firehose to ingest the dataset from S3 and transform it into RecordIO-Protobuf format.

A logistics company is optimizing delivery routes using historical shipment data but faces missing values in its dataset. The training data is stored in Amazon Redshift, and the company plans to perform data preprocessing and feature engineering entirely within the Amazon SageMaker AI environment to streamline its model development workflow.

What is the MOST operationally efficient way to handle the missing values in their dataset?

- Export the data from Amazon Redshift to Amazon S3. Use Amazon Athena to preprocess the data and handle missing values before importing it back into SageMaker AI.
- **Perform data preprocessing using SageMaker Data Wrangler built-in features within Amazon SageMaker Canvas to handle missing values.**
    
    Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler, integrated with Amazon SageMaker Canvas, offers a powerful solution for simplifying data preparation and feature engineering tasks. SageMaker Canvas is a no-code visual interface that enables users to perform machine learning tasks without needing to write any code. SageMaker Data Wrangler provides over 300 built-in data transformations within this environment, allowing users to efficiently clean, transform, and handle missing values in their datasets. Combining Data Wrangler’s capabilities with SageMaker Canvas will enable users to perform complex feature engineering tasks, such as filling in missing values, normalizing data, and encoding categorical variables, all within an intuitive, user-friendly interface. This integration helps streamline the data preparation, enabling teams to focus on model building and deployment.
    
    ![Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-data-wrangler-08-19-2024.png)
    
    SageMaker Canvas and Data Wrangler enable a seamless transition from data preprocessing to model development. After performing data transformations and handling missing values, users can export the cleaned data directly into SageMaker for further analysis, training, and deployment of machine learning models. The visual interface of SageMaker Canvas also allows for easy collaboration between data scientists and business analysts, reducing the need for complex data pipelines and minimizing operational overhead. By leveraging these tools within the SageMaker environment, organizations can significantly enhance their machine learning workflows, ensuring data consistency and efficiency throughout the pipeline.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Perform data preprocessing using SageMaker Data Wrangler built-in features within Amazon SageMaker Canvas to handle missing values.**
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Redshift’s built-in SQL functions to handle missing values directly within the Redshift cluster before exporting the data to Amazon SageMaker AI for model development** is incorrect. While Amazon Redshift has powerful SQL capabilities to handle missing values, allowing you to preprocess the data within the data warehouse itself, this approach still requires transferring the preprocessed data to Amazon SageMaker AI for model development. This extra step of data movement simply increases operational complexity and time. It is more efficient to handle preprocessing directly within the Amazon SageMaker environment, where the model development will occur. Thus, it streamlines the workflow and reduces unnecessary data transfers.
    
    The option that says: **Export the data from Amazon Redshift to Amazon S3. Use Amazon Athena to preprocess the data and handle missing values before importing it back into SageMaker AI**is incorrect. This option involves multiple data transfers in which these steps only add operational overhead, increase latency, and complicate the workflow. SageMaker Data Wrangler, on the other hand, provides a more seamless and efficient way to handle missing values within the same environment.
    
    The option that says: **Create a Lambda function to identify and fill in missing values in the dataset automatically** is incorrect. While AWS Lambda can be used for data processing tasks, including filling in missing values, this approach requires custom code and setup, adding complexity to the solution. It also does not use the built-in data preprocessing capabilities of SageMaker Data Wrangler, which is specifically designed for these types of tasks.
    
- Create a Lambda function to identify and fill in missing values in the dataset automatically.
- Use Amazon Redshift's built-in SQL functions to handle missing values directly within the Redshift cluster before exporting the data to Amazon SageMaker AI for model development.

A financial services company is building a customer support chatbot using retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). The chatbot’s knowledge base must leverage Amazon Bedrock and include sanitized transaction records stored in Amazon S3. The company wants to ensure that all data used by the chatbot is compliant with financial regulations and easily accessible for queries.

Choose and arrange the appropriate steps from the list below to build the chatbot’s knowledge base for RAG. Each step can be selected once or not at all. (Select and order THREE.)

- Establish an Amazon RDS instance to hold the transaction data.
- Load the information into the knowledge base.
- Set up an Amazon S3 bucket.
- Set up an Amazon SNS topic to handle data alerts.
- Configure a knowledge base in Amazon Bedrock and link it to the Amazon S3 bucket as the data source.
- **Answer**
    
    Step 1 - Set up an Amazon S3 bucket
    Step 2 - Configure a knowledge base in Amazon Bedrock and choose the Amazon S3 bucket as the data source
    
    Step 3 - Load the information into the knowledge base
    
    **Amazon S3** serves as a durable and scalable storage solution that is commonly used for storing vast amounts of structured and unstructured data. In this scenario, creating an Amazon S3 bucket is essential for storing the sanitized transaction records that will be used to build the knowledge base for the chatbot.
    
    **Amazon Bedrock** is a service that enables the development and deployment of applications that use machine learning models. By creating and configuring a knowledge base in Amazon Bedrock, you can ensure that the data stored in the S3 bucket is utilized effectively as the foundation for the chatbot’s knowledge base.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-Amazon-BedRock-&-Amazon-S3-20Aug2024.png)
    
    Once the S3 bucket is created and linked to Amazon Bedrock, loading the data into the knowledge base is the final step. This allows the knowledge base to access the necessary information, making it readily available for the chatbot to handle customer queries and provide accurate responses.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **Step 1: Set up an Amazon S3 bucket.**
    
    **Step 2: Configure a knowledge base in Amazon Bedrock and link it to the Amazon S3 bucket as the data source.**
    
    **Step 3: Load the information into the knowledge base.**
    
    The option that says: **Establish an Amazon RDS instance to hold the transaction data** is incorrect because RDS is not a valid data source for Amazon Bedrock.
    
    The option that says: **Set up an Amazon SNS topic to handle data alerts** is incorrect because this option is primarily used for sending notifications, not for building or linking knowledge bases
    

A healthcare technology company is developing a machine learning model to analyze medical records. These records are in PDF format and stored in an Amazon S3 bucket. These records contain sensitive information such as patient names, addresses, phone numbers, and medical history details. Before training the model, the company must detect and redact personally identifiable information (PII) and protected health information (PHI) from the records to ensure compliance with data privacy regulations.

Which is the most operationally efficient solution that can meet the requirements?

- Use S3 Object Lambda with Comprehend to redact PII/PHI.
- Convert PDFs to text, then use Comprehend to detect and redact PII/PHI.
- Use Amazon Textract to extract text from PDFs, then use a SageMaker JumpStart pre-trained model for redaction.
- **Use a SageMaker Canvas ready-to-use model to process PDFs and redact PII/PHI.**
    
    **Amazon SageMaker Canvas** is a no-code visual interface that empowers you to prepare data, build, and deploy highly accurate ML models, streamlining the end-to-end ML lifecycle in a unified environment. With SageMaker Canvas, users can easily import data from various sources, visualize and prepare the data, and then train, evaluate, and deploy ML models using a simple drag-and-drop interface. SageMaker Canvas leverages Amazon SageMaker’s robust ML infrastructure to provide an intuitive platform for creating predictive models, making machine learning more accessible to a broader audience, including those without deep technical expertise.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-amazon-sagemaker-canvas-20-Aug-2024.png)
    
    Using **Amazon SageMaker Canvas** eliminates the need for extensive configuration, manual intervention, or the setup of additional services, thus significantly reducing operational overhead. It includes ready-to-use models that can automatically detect and redact PII and PHI from text data. This solution requires the least operational overhead because you can use a pre-built model without developing your code or converting the PDF files to text format.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use a SageMaker Canvas ready-to-use model to process PDFs and redact PII/PHI.**
    
    The option that says: **Use S3 Object Lambda with Comprehend to redact PII/PHI** is incorrect. While Amazon Comprehend can detect and redact PII and PHI from text data, using it with an Amazon S3 Object Lambda access point would only introduce additional operational overhead. This approach would require configuring, deploying, and maintaining a Lambda function, which goes against the requirement of minimal operational overhead.
    
    The option that says: **Convert PDFs to text, then use Comprehend to detect and redact PII/PHI** is incorrect. While Amazon Comprehend can primarily detect and redact PII and PHI from text data, it cannot process PDF files directly. The PDF files would need to be converted to text format first, adding operational overhead compared to using a SageMaker Canvas ready-to-use model that can process PDF files directly.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Textract to extract text from PDFs, then use a SageMaker JumpStart pre-trained model for redaction** is incorrect. While Amazon Textract can extract text from PDF files, and SageMaker JumpStart provides pre-trained models, this solution requires more operational overhead than a SageMaker Canvas ready-to-use model. You would need to configure and deploy the pre-trained model and integrate it with Amazon Textract.
    

A data science team is developing multiple machine learning (ML) applications that require both training and low-latency inference. These applications need to share features across different AWS accounts with minimal operational overhead.

Which of the following options will fulfill the given requirements?

- Store features in Amazon S3 and use AWS Glue to catalog and share features across accounts.
- **Use Amazon SageMaker Feature Store with both online and offline stores, and configure cross-account access to share features.**
    
    The process of developing machine learning (ML) involves extracting raw data and converting it into features, which are meaningful inputs for your ML model. These features are then stored in a way that allows for data exploration, ML training, and ML inference. Amazon SageMaker Feature Store makes it easier for you to create, store, share, and manage features by providing options for feature stores and reducing the need for repetitive data processing and curation work.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/sagemaker-feature-store-091624.png)
    
    Amazon SageMaker Feature Store is specifically designed for storing, sharing, and managing features for machine learning models. It supports both online and offline stores, which cater to low-latency inference and training needs, respectively. Additionally, it allows for cross-account access, making it easy to share features across different AWS accounts with minimal operational overhead.
    
    **– Online Store**: Provides low-latency access to features for real-time inference.
    
    **– Offline Store**: Used for batch processing and training.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use Amazon SageMaker Feature Store with both online and offline stores, and configure cross-account access to share features.**
    
    The option that says: **Store features in Amazon S3 and use AWS Glue to catalog and share features across accounts** is incorrect. While Amazon S3 and AWS Glue can be used to store and catalog features, this setup does not inherently support low-latency inference. AWS Glue is typically more suited for ETL (Extract, Transform, Load) operations and data cataloging, which might introduce additional latency and complexity in managing access policies across accounts.
    
    The option that says: **Store features in Amazon S3 and use AWS IAM to manually manage access policies for each account** is incorrect because Storing features in Amazon S3 and manually managing access policies with AWS IAM can be more complex and error-prone. This approach requires significant operational overhead to ensure proper access controls and does not provide the integrated feature management capabilities that SageMaker Feature Store offers.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon RDS to store features and set up replication between databases in different accounts** is incorrect because Amazon RDS is primarily a relational database service and is not optimized for storing and managing machine learning features. Setting up replication between databases in different accounts can be complex and does not address the need for low-latency inference. This option also lacks the specialized feature management capabilities provided by SageMaker Feature Store.
    
- Store features in Amazon S3 and use AWS IAM to manually manage access policies for each account.
- Use Amazon RDS to store features and set up replication between databases in different accounts.

An ML Specialist is deploying an updated XGBoost machine learning model to detect fraudulent transactions. To minimize disruption and ensure a smooth rollout, the specialist begins by deploying the new model to a small percentage of transactions while continuing to process the majority with the old model.

What deployment strategy must the specialist use to ensure a controlled and safe transition?

- Blue/green deployment
- **Canary deployment**
    
    **Canary deployment** is a method that allows you to deploy new versions of machine learning models or applications to a small subset of users or traffic. At the same time, the majority continues to use the existing version. This deployment strategy enables you to monitor the performance and behavior of the new version on a limited scale, minimizing the risk of widespread issues. By observing metrics and feedback from the canary deployment, you can identify potential problems early and make adjustments before rolling out the new version to the entire user base. This approach is beneficial for ensuring a smooth transition, as it provides the flexibility to roll back to the previous version if any issues are detected.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-canary-deployment-08-21-2024.png)
    
    Canary deployments can be implemented using Amazon SageMaker for machine learning models. SageMaker allows you to deploy models in production using canary or linear deployment strategies, ensuring controlled updates with minimal disruption. With canary deployment, you can gradually shift traffic to the new model, monitoring performance metrics and making informed decisions about when to fully deploy the new version. This method supports efficient model management, reducing the risk of errors and ensuring that only well-tested models are deployed to all users.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Canary deployment.**
    
    The option that says: **Blue/green deployment** is incorrect. This option involves switching all traffic from the old version (blue) to the new version (green) at once after full testing. This method does not align with the phased approach, where the new model initially handles only a small percentage of transactions.
    
    The option that says: **Linear deployment** is incorrect. This deployment strategy gradually shifts traffic steadily from the old version to the new version over time. While this method allows for a controlled transition, it does not provide the initial small-scale rollout and evaluation phase that Canary deployment offers, which is crucial in the scenario to minimize disruption and allow for immediate rollback if issues are detected.
    
    The option that says: **Shadow deployment** is incorrect because this just runs the new version alongside the old version for testing without affecting live traffic. This method is primarily useful for testing but does not involve processing a small percentage of live transactions with the new model.
    
- Linear deployment
- Shadow deployment

A data scientist working with Amazon SageMaker Feature Store is required to incorporate a new feature into an existing feature group that contains historical data. The feature group is designed to support several machine learning models. The data scientist must ensure that the new feature is added and that all records, including historical ones, reflect this new feature.

Which two actions should the data scientist perform? (Select TWO.)

- Execute the `DeleteFeatureGroup` command to remove the existing feature group.
- Set the new feature as the primary key to update all records automatically.
- **Utilize the `UpdateFeatureGroup` command to incorporate the new feature into the feature group. Specify the attribute name and type.**
- Reindex the feature group to apply the new feature across all records.
- **Apply the `PutRecord` command to update the records that are missing data for the new attribute.**
- **Evaluation**
    
    You can update and describe your feature group, as well as add features and records, through the Amazon SageMaker Feature Store API console. A feature group is an object that holds your data, while a feature describes a column in the database. When you add a feature to the feature group, you’re essentially adding a column to the table. When you add a new record to the feature group, you are filling up values for features that are associated with a specific record ID.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/TD_Amazon-SageMaker-Feature-Store-API-21Aug2024.jpg)
    
    To modify an existing feature group in the Amazon SageMaker Feature Store, you can use the `UpdateFeatureGroup` command to introduce new features. This operation allows you to add additional columns (features) to the feature group, ensuring that the feature group can evolve as your data requirements change. After adding the new feature, you can use the `PutRecord API` to update the records within the feature group. The `PutRecord` API enables you to ingest or overwrite records, ensuring that the newly added feature has data populated across both historical and new records.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Utilize the `UpdateFeatureGroup`command to incorporate the new feature into the feature group. Specify the attribute name and type.**
    
    **– Apply the `PutRecord` command to update the records that are missing data for the new attribute.**
    
    The option that says: **Execute the `DeleteFeatureGroup` command to remove the existing feature group** is incorrect because deleting the feature group would only result in the loss of all existing records, including historical data. The requirement is to maintain these records while adding a new feature. Removing the feature group contradicts this objective, as it would erase the entire feature group and its contents. Instead, the feature group should be updated without deletion.
    
    The option that says: **Set the new feature as the primary key to update all records automatically** is incorrect because setting a new attribute as the primary key would not automatically update all records with the new attribute. Primary keys are unique identifiers that cannot be easily changed or used to update records retrospectively.
    
    The option that says: **Reindex the feature group to apply the new feature across all records** is incorrect because reindexing is simply not a valid operation in Amazon SageMaker Feature Store. You must manually update records using the appropriate commands, such as PutRecord, rather than relying on a reindexing operation.
    

A company is training a machine learning model on a dataset containing sensitive customer information. Due to data privacy regulations, the training process must be isolated from external networks.

What solution would meet the requirements?

- Use SageMaker Canvas and configure a VPC interface endpoint to block internet access.
- **Enable SageMaker AI network isolation mode.**
    
    Amazon SageMaker AI provides various security features to ensure the privacy and isolation of training data and models. SageMaker’s network isolation mode allows you to configure your training jobs and hosted models to run without internet access, ensuring complete network isolation.
    
    ![sagemaker-network-isolation](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-sagemaker-network-isolation-21-Aug-2024.png)
    
    To meet the requirement of isolating the training process from external networks, **SageMaker’s network isolation mode** is an effective solution. Network isolation mode ensures that the training container does not have access to the internet during the training process, thus fully isolating the model training from external networks. This option addresses both data privacy regulations and the need to minimize operational overhead.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Enable SageMaker AI network isolation mode.**
    
    The option that says: **Use SageMaker’s built-in encryption at rest and in transit** is incorrect because this option alone is not sufficient to ensure that the training data remains secure and isolated from external networks. While SageMaker provides encryption at rest and in transit, it does not inherently isolate the training process from external networks. Additional measures are required to achieve network isolation.
    
    The option that says: **Use SageMaker Canvas and configure a VPC interface endpoint to block internet access** is incorrect because SageMaker Canvas is just a low-code/no-code interface primarily for building machine learning models, and it does not provide network isolation capabilities. Additionally, while VPC interface endpoints can be used to restrict internet access, SageMaker Canvas won’t guarantee that the training process is completely isolated from external networks, as there could be underlying connections or API calls that still communicate with the outside world.
    
    The option that says: **Store the training data in an encrypted S3 bucket with restricted access** is incorrect because this is crucial for securing data at rest but does not address the network isolation needed during the training process. Encryption and access restrictions typically do not prevent the training environment from potentially accessing or communicating over external networks.
    
- Use SageMaker's built-in encryption at rest and in transit.
- Store the training data in an encrypted S3 bucket with restricted access

A team is currently working on a project that involves gathering real-time data from thousands of IoT sensors deployed across a widespread network. These sensors monitor various parameters, including air quality, traffic flow, and energy consumption. The goal is to develop a system capable of identifying abnormalities in this continuous data stream.

Which of the following AWS services are best suited for implementing the real-time anomaly detection system described in the scenario? (Select TWO.)

- **Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink**
- Amazon SageMaker AI
- Amazon Data Firehose
- AWS Lambda
- **Amazon Kinesis Data Streams**
- **Evaluation**
    
    **Amazon Kinesis Data Streams** is a service designed to handle high-throughput, continuous data streams from various sources, including IoT sensors. It allows you to ingest, process, and analyze real-time data. In the given scenario, Kinesis Data Streams is crucial for ingesting sensor data. This service provides a scalable and durable platform for managing large volumes of streaming data. You can create data streams, partition data, and distribute it across multiple consumers (e.g., Flink applications). Real-time processing applications can subscribe to these streams to perform analytics.
    
    ![amazon-kinesis-data-stream](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/amazon-kinesis-data-stream-08-21-24.png)
    
    **Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink,** formerly known as Amazon Kinesis Data Analytics, is a fully managed service that operates Apache Flink applications. In the given scenario, it processes the incoming data stream from Kinesis Data Streams. Flink is a robust stream processing framework that supports complex event-time processing, windowing, and stateful computations. It enables you to create customized Flink applications (using Flink SQL or Flink DataStream APIs) for real-time data analysis. In this setup, the customized Flink application aggregates sensor data, computes statistical metrics, and identifies anomalies.
    
    ![amazon-managed-service-apache-flink](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/amazon-managed-service-apache-flink-08-21-24.png)
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Amazon Kinesis Data Streams.**
    
    **– Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink.**
    
    The option that says: **Amazon SageMaker AI** is incorrect because this is a service primarily used for building, training, and deploying machine learning models, not for real-time stream processing.
    
    The option that says: **Amazon Data Firehose** is incorrect. Firehose is primarily used for loading data into data lakes, data warehouses, or other storage destinations (e.g., Amazon S3, Redshift). It doesn’t perform real-time processing or anomaly detection. Firehose is more about data delivery and transformation.
    
    The option that says: **AWS Lambda** is incorrect because this is just a serverless compute service that executes code in response to events (e.g., changes in S3 buckets, API Gateway requests). It is not typically designed for stream processing or anomaly detection. Lambda functions are stateless and short-lived, making them less suitable for continuous stream processing.
    

A logistics company relies on a 2 GB custom machine learning model hosted in an on-premises environment to forecast delivery times. The company needs to migrate this model to AWS to lower operational overhead and use a fully managed service. The model experiences unpredictable traffic with peak loads during business hours and low activity at night and on weekends. The solution must scale automatically based on demand and be cost-effective during idle times.

Which AWS service is the most suitable for these requirements?

- **Deploy the model as a serverless SageMaker AI endpoint without Provisioned Concurrency.**
    
    **Amazon SageMaker Serverless Inference** is a purpose-built inference option that enables you to deploy and scale ML models without configuring or managing any of the underlying infrastructure. On-demand Serverless Inference is ideal for workloads that have idle periods between traffic spurts and can tolerate cold starts. Serverless endpoints automatically launch compute resources and scale them in and out depending on traffic, eliminating the need to choose instance types or manage scaling policies. This takes away the undifferentiated heavy lifting of selecting and managing servers.
    
    ![sagemaker-serverless-inference-endpoint](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-sagemaker-serverless-inference-endpoint-16-Aug-2024.png)
    
    With SageMaker Serverless Inference, you pay only for the duration of running the inference code and the amount of data processed, not for idle time. This makes it a cost-effective option for workloads with intermittent or unpredictable traffic. You can easily create a SageMaker Serverless Inference endpoint using the SageMaker console, AWS SDKs, the Amazon SageMaker Python SDK, or the AWS CLI. This allows you to focus on your machine learning model rather than managing the underlying infrastructure. Lastly, SageMaker Serverless Inference is well-suited for applications with unpredictable or intermittent traffic patterns, such as chatbots or other services that experience fluctuations in demand.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Deploy the model as a serverless SageMaker AI endpoint without Provisioned Concurrency.**
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon SageMaker AI real-time endpoints with auto-scaling enabled** is incorrect. While SageMaker AI real-time endpoints can automatically scale based on demand, they are not designed to minimize costs during idle times. Real-time endpoints typically keep instances running even when there is no traffic, which can lead to higher costs during low-activity periods.
    
    The option that says: **Deploy the model as a serverless SageMaker AI endpoint with Provisioned Concurrency** is incorrect because Provisioned Concurrency ensures that the model is always available, even during spikes in traffic, but this can only lead to higher costs during idle periods due to reserved capacity.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Lambda to host the model, loading it from Amazon S3 on each invocation** is incorrect because the model size exceeds Lambda’s deployment limits.
    
- Use AWS Lambda to host the model, loading it from Amazon S3 on each invocation.
- Deploy the model as a serverless SageMaker AI endpoint with Provisioned Concurrency.
- Use Amazon SageMaker AI real-time endpoints with auto-scaling enabled.

A manufacturing company is developing a customer segmentation model using Amazon SageMaker AI. The company has a tabular dataset with nominal categorical variables such as customer region and product preferences, as well as numerical variables like purchase frequency and total spending stored in an Amazon S3 bucket. The ML engineer needs to preprocess the dataset to ensure it can be effectively analyzed and used for model training in SageMaker.

Which Amazon SageMaker AI feature will help the engineer fulfill the requirements?

- **Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler**
    
    **Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler** is a powerful tool designed to simplify and streamline the process of preparing data for machine learning. It allows users to import data from various sources, including Amazon S3, Amazon Athena, and Redshift, and provides an intuitive interface for cleaning, transforming, and visualizing data. With over 300 built-in data transformations, SageMaker Data Wrangler enables data scientists and engineers to perform tasks such as encoding categorical variables, scaling numerical data, handling missing values, and more without needing to write complex code. This functionality significantly reduces the time and effort required to prepare data for model training.
    
    ![Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/data-wrangler-070524.png)
    
    Additionally, SageMaker Data Wrangler integrates seamlessly with other SageMaker services, making it easier to transition from data preparation to model training and deployment. Users can create processing workflows, visualize data transformations, and even analyze the importance of features within the same environment. This holistic approach to data preparation ensures that models are trained on well-prepared datasets, ultimately leading to more accurate and reliable predictions.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler.**
    
    The option that says: **Amazon SageMaker Feature Store** is incorrect because it is primarily designed to store, retrieve, and share machine learning features. While it helps manage features for consistent model training and inference, it does not offer extensive data preprocessing capabilities for transforming categorical and numerical variables.
    
    The option that says: **Amazon SageMaker JumpStart** is incorrect. This feature only provides pre-built models and end-to-end solutions for common machine learning use cases, allowing users to quickly start their ML projects. However, it does not focus on the data preparation tasks required in this scenario, such as encoding categorical variables or normalizing numerical data.
    
    The option that says: **Amazon SageMaker Neo** is incorrect because it is used to optimize machine learning models for inference on different hardware platforms, including edge devices. While it enhances models’ performance after training, it does not provide tools for preprocessing data or transforming variables before model training, which is the primary requirement in this scenario.
    
- Amazon SageMaker Neo
- Amazon SageMaker JumpStart
- Amazon SageMaker Feature Store

A financial services company uses 5 ML models to detect fraudulent transactions in real-time. The data team processes daily transaction streams for retraining to improve model accuracy. The company aims to create a centralized Amazon SageMaker Feature Store to organize and distribute attributes among different models and departments.

Select and order the correct steps from the list below to develop and distribute features. Each step should be selected one time or not at all. (Select and order THREE.)

- Connect with an offline store to the feature group.
- Schedule regular feature updates
- Define a new feature group
- Create an endpoint for feature queries
- Connect with an online store to the feature group.
- Load data into the offline store using batch processing.
- **Answer**
    
    Step 1 - Define a new feature group
    
    Step 2 - Connect with an offline store to the feature group. 
    
    Step 3 - Load data into the offline store using batch processing
    
    Amazon SageMaker Feature Store is a fully managed, specialized service designed to store, share, and manage features for machine learning (ML) models. Features are the data inputs used by ML models during both the training and inference stages.
    
    Feature group is the primary resource within Feature Store that holds both the data and metadata used for training or making predictions with an ML model. A feature group represents a logical collection of features that describe individual records. In the example diagram below, a feature group encompasses your ML data.
    
    Offline store retains historical data in an Amazon S3 bucket. It is intended for scenarios where low-latency (sub-second) reads are unnecessary. For instance, the offline store can be utilized to store and deliver features for data exploration, model training, and batch inference tasks.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/TD-Amazon-SageMaker-feature-store_feature-group-22Aug2024.jpg)
    
    The correct steps to set up a centralized Amazon SageMaker Feature Store for managing features in machine learning (ML) models involve first defining a feature group, which organizes the features and metadata for model training and inference. Next, the feature group is linked to an offline store, which stores historical data in Amazon S3 and supports batch processing. Finally, data is loaded into the offline store in batch mode, enabling the company to efficiently manage and retrain ML models with updated features, improving accuracy and performance.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Step 1: Define a new feature group.**
    
    **– Step 2: Connect with an offline store to the feature group.**
    
    **– Step 3: Load data into the offline store using batch processing.**
    
    The option that says: **Schedule regular feature updates** is incorrect because this option would be a separate maintenance task. Therefore, this is not part of the initial feature store setup process.
    
    The option that says: **Create an endpoint for feature queries** is incorrect because this option is only related to querying features during inference, not part of the feature store setup for retraining.
    
    The option that says: **Connect with an online store to the feature group** is incorrect. Online stores are typically used for low-latency access during inference, not for retraining models as described in the scenario.
    

A machine learning team at a retail company is developing a model to predict customer churn. The model will identify customers who are likely to stop using the company’s services in the next 30 days. The team is concerned about the model’s interpretability and potential biases in its predictions.

Choose the appropriate explainability analysis technique for each team’s requirements from the following list. Each technique may be selected once or multiple times. (Select TWO.)

- Identify how the predicted probability of churn changes as customer age increases.
    - **Kullback-Leibler Divergence (KL)**
    - **Total Variation Distance (TVD)**
    - **Difference in proportions of labels (DPL)**
    - **Partial dependence plots (PDPs)**
- Measure the imbalance in the predicted churn rates between different customer demographic groups (e.g., age groups).
    - **Kullback-Leibler Divergence (KL)**
    - **Partial dependence plots (PDPs)**
    - **Difference in proportions of labels (DPL)**
    - **Total Variation Distance (TVD)**
- **Answer**
    
    **Partial Dependence Plots (PDPs)** are a popular technique for interpreting machine learning models by visualizing the marginal effect of one or more features on the model’s predictions.
    
    In the given scenario, PDPs can be used to “identify how the predicted probability of churn changes as customer age increases.” This technique can help the team understand the relationship between customer age and the predicted churn probability, which can be useful for identifying potential biases or patterns in the model’s predictions.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-DPL-explainability-analysis-technique-26-08-24.png)
    
    **Difference in Proportions of Labels (DPL)**
    
    is a metric used to measure the difference in the proportions of positive and negative labels between two groups.
    
    In the given scenario, DPL can be used to “measure the imbalance in the predicted churn rates between different customer demographic groups (e.g., age groups).” This technique can help the team identify potential biases or disparities in the model’s predictions across different demographic groups, which is a crucial consideration for ensuring fairness and avoiding discrimination.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-DPL-explainability-analysis-technique-2-26-08-24.png)
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    – Identify how the predicted probability of churn changes as customer age increases: **Partial dependence plots (PDPs)**
    
    – Measure the imbalance in the predicted churn rates between different customer demographic groups (e.g., age groups): **Difference in proportions of labels (DPL)**
    
    The option that says: **Total Variation Distance (TVD)** is incorrect. Total Variation Distance (TVD) is a metric primarily used to measure the overall difference between two probability distributions.
    
    The option that says: **Kullback-Leibler Divergence (KL)** is incorrect. Kullback-Leibler Divergence (KL) is a metric typically used to measure the difference between two probability distributions, similar to Total Variation Distance.
    

An ML Manager is analyzing both categorical and numeric features in a dataset using Amazon SageMaker Studio Notebook. The analyst needs to choose the appropriate statistical test or correlation metric to evaluate non-linear relationships between features.

Which statistical test or correlation metric should the analyst use?

- **Spearman**
    
    **Spearman’s correlation** is particularly useful when analyzing relationships between numeric features where the relationship might not be linear or when the data does not meet the assumptions of normality. Since Spearman’s correlation is a non-parametric measure, it determines the strength and direction of a monotonic relationship between two variables without assuming a particular distribution. This makes it suitable for both continuous and ordinal data, which is ideal for datasets that include a mix of numeric and categorical features.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/TD-Spearman's-Rank-Correlatation-Coefficient-26Aug2024.jpg)
    
    In this scenario, if the relationship between the numeric features is expected to be non-linear or if the data includes ranks rather than raw values, **Spearman’s correlation** would be the appropriate choice for evaluating these relationships.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Spearman.**
    
    The option that says: **Cramér’s V** is incorrect because this option is only ideal for measuring the association between two categorical variables and is not applicable for evaluating relationships between numeric features.
    
    The option that says: **Kendall’s Tau** is incorrect. Although it is also a non-parametric correlation metric like Spearman, it is generally used to measure the ordinal association between two variables. Kendall’s Tau is often preferred when dealing with small sample sizes and ordinal data. Therefore, while Kendall’s Tau is effective in certain cases, Spearman’s rank correlation is more appropriate for this scenario.
    
    The option that says: **Pearson** is incorrect because this correlation coefficient only measures the strength and direction of a linear relationship between two numeric variables. Pearson’s correlation assumes normality and linearity, making it unsuitable for analyzing categorical features or non-linear relationships. In scenarios where non-linear relationships might exist, Spearman’s rank correlation is more appropriate.
    
- Cramér's V
- Kendall's Tau
- Pearson

An ML engineer is overseeing the deployment of a machine learning (ML) project and aims to streamline the process of building, packaging, and deploying their code. The engineer wants to use AWS resources to manage various development lifecycle stages.

Match each AWS service with the task it best suits in the ML project deployment process. Each service must be selected only once. (Select FOUR.)

- AWS CodeArtifact
- AWS CodeDeploy
- AWS CodeBuild
- AWS CodePipeline
- It helps to avoid downtime during deployment.
    - **AWS CodeDeploy**
    - AWS CodePipeline
    - AWS CodeBuild
    - AWS CodeArtifact
- Build and test the code.
    - AWS CodeDeploy
    - AWS CodePipeline
    - AWS CodeArtifact
    - **AWS CodeBuild**
- Store and manage the built packages.
    - AWS CodePipeline
    - **AWS CodeArtifact**
    - AWS CodeDeploy
    - AWS CodeBuild
- Automate the build, test, and deployment workflow.
    - AWS CodeDeploy
    - AWS CodeArtifact
    - **AWS CodePipeline**
    - AWS CodeBuild
- **Evaluation**
    
    **AWS CodeDeploy** is essential for deploying machine learning (ML) projects. It provides an automated and efficient way to deploy ML models and applications across various environments. This tool allows for deploying updated ML models to different instances, such as EC2, Lambda, or on-premises servers, with minimal downtime and reduced risk of errors. CodeDeploy seamlessly integrates with other AWS services, enabling smooth transitions between different stages of the ML lifecycle, from development and testing to production. By automating deployment processes and managing version control, AWS CodeDeploy ensures that the latest model updates are consistently and reliably applied, thus supporting continuous delivery and operational efficiency of ML applications.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-aws-codedeploy-09-02-2024.png)
    
    **AWS CodeBuild** is a service that compiles source code, runs tests, and produces software packages ready for deployment. It can scale and process multiple builds concurrently, reducing the time needed to build and test code. CodeBuild is used to build and test code. Developers can configure CodeBuild to automate the process of compiling code and running tests, ensuring the code is ready for production before deployment.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-AWS%20CodeBuild-08-27-2024.png)
    
    **AWS CodeArtifact** is a service for storing, publishing, and sharing software packages used in development. It supports various package formats such as Maven, npm, and PyPI. This service is used to store and manage built packages. Once CodeBuild compiles the code, the resulting artifacts can be securely stored and shared in CodeArtifact while maintaining different versions.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-AWS%20CodeArtifact-08-27-2024.png)
    
    **AWS CodePipeline** is a service that facilitates continuous integration and continuous delivery (CI/CD) for quick and dependable updates to applications and infrastructure. It automates the release process’s build, test, and deploy phases and orchestrates the CI/CD pipeline. CodePipeline streamlines the workflow for building, testing, and deploying code changes, guaranteeing a seamless and automated process from development to production.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-AWS%20CodePipeline-08-27-2024.png)
    
    Various AWS services are essential in managing the code lifecycle when deploying a machine learning project. AWS CodeDeploy helps users to minimize downtime and reduce the risk of errors when deploying the ML models. AWS CodeBuild handles building and testing the code, ensuring it is production-ready. AWS CodeArtifact stores and manages the built packages, making them available for deployment. Finally, AWS CodePipeline orchestrates the entire CI/CD workflow, automating the build, test, and deployment stages to streamline the release process. Together, these services enable a seamless and efficient approach to deploying ML projects into production.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    – It helps to avoid downtime during deployment: **AWS CodeDeploy**
    
    – Build and test the code: **AWS CodeBuild**
    
    – Store and manage the built packages: **AWS CodeArtifact**
    
    **–** Automate the build, test, and deployment workflow: **AWS CodePipeline**
    

A real estate organization aims to create an ML model with Amazon SageMaker AI to forecast housing prices across different cities. The dataset contains a significant number of records with features such as square footage, number of bedrooms, location, and year built. The data scientist must select the appropriate algorithm for regression-based predictions.

Which SageMaker AI built-in algorithm would be most appropriate for this scenario?

- Random Cut Forest (RCF) algorithm
- Amazon SageMaker DeepAR forecasting algorithm
- Principal Component Analysis (PCA) algorithm
- **Linear learner algorithm**
    
    **Linear models** are types of supervised learning algorithms that help make predictions based on labeled data. In simpler terms, the model is given examples where each example has some features (like size, price, or age) and an outcome (like a house price or a category). For classification tasks, the model sorts data into categories, such as whether a house is expensive or not. For regression tasks, it predicts a specific value, like the actual price of a house. The goal of the model is to learn a straight-line relationship between the input features and the predicted outcome.
    
    ![Linear Learner Algorithm](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/Linear-Learner-Algorithm-16Sept2024.jpg)
    
    The **Linear Learner algorithm** is the most appropriate choice for this scenario. The Linear Learner algorithm in Amazon SageMaker is designed for both regression and classification tasks. It is well-suited for problems where the goal is to predict a continuous variable, such as housing prices, based on various features like square footage, number of bedrooms, location, and year built. By learning a linear function that maps input features to the predicted output, this algorithm can effectively model the relationships between these features and the target variable, which in this case is the housing price.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Linear learner algorithm.**
    
    The option that says: **Random Cut Forest (RCF) algorithm** is incorrect because it is only used for anomaly detection, identifying outliers in a dataset rather than making regression-based predictions.
    
    The option that says: **Amazon SageMaker DeepAR forecasting algorithm** is incorrect because this is simply designed for time series forecasting, which focuses on predicting future values based on temporal data. While powerful for forecasting tasks, it is not the best fit for regression problems like predicting housing prices from a set of features.
    
    The option that says: **Principal Component Analysis (PCA) algorithm** is incorrect because this is just a dimensionality reduction technique that helps in reducing the number of features by finding the most important ones. However, it does not directly address regression tasks or make predictions.
    

A logistics company wants to improve its internal support system by implementing a smart assistant chatbot to help employees quickly find answers to complex operational procedures and protocols. The company’s documents contain PDF files stored in an Amazon S3 bucket. The chatbot will leverage a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) approach using Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate responses.

Which solution should the ML engineer use to make the operational guidelines searchable by the RAG chatbot? (Select TWO.)

- **Ingest the PDF files into an Amazon OpenSearch Service vector database.**
- Convert the PDF files to text and store them in an Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL database.
- Use AWS Glue to transform the PDF files into a structured dataset and store them in an Amazon RDS instance.
- Use Amazon Comprehend to extract entities and key phrases from the PDF files and storing them in an Amazon DynamoDB table.
- **Create a knowledge base in Amazon Bedrock with the PDF files stored in the Amazon S3 bucket as the data source.**
- **Evaluation**
    
    **Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG)** is a technique used in natural language processing (NLP) that combines the strengths of retrieval-based and generation-based models. It involves retrieving relevant information from a knowledge base and then using a language model to generate a response based on the retrieved information.
    
    **Amazon OpenSearch Service** can be utilized as a vector database, which is ideal for storing and retrieving high-dimensional vectors needed for RAG applications. This enables the chatbot to efficiently search and retrieve relevant document vectors that contain the necessary operational guidelines.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-opensearch-27-08-2024.png)
    
    **Amazon Bedrock** is a fully managed service that enables developers to build and deploy machine learning models for natural language processing (NLP) tasks, including question-answering and information retrieval. Bedrock can ingest data from various sources, including Amazon S3 buckets, and create a knowledge base that can be used by the RAG chatbot to retrieve relevant information from the operational guidelines.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-Amazon-BedRock-%26-Amazon-S3-20Aug2024.png)
    
    In a RAG setup, the retrieval process involves querying the **Amazon OpenSearch Service vector database** with a natural language query, using vector similarity search to find the most relevant sections of documents. These retrieved document vectors are then passed to a **large language model (LLM)** for generating the final response. Similarly, the **knowledge base created in Amazon Bedrock** can be queried by the retrieval component to identify the most relevant operational procedures, which are then used by the LLM to craft a response.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Ingest the PDF files into an Amazon OpenSearch Service vector database.**
    
    **– Create a knowledge base in Amazon Bedrock with the PDF files stored in the Amazon S3 bucket as the data source.**
    
    The option that says: **Convert the PDF files to text and store them in an Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL database** is incorrect. While Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL is a fully managed relational database service, it is not optimized for full-text search and retrieval tasks required by the RAG chatbot. Storing the converted text files in a relational database would typically make it difficult to efficiently search and retrieve relevant information for the chatbot.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Glue to transform the PDF files into a structured dataset and store them in an Amazon RDS instance** is incorrect because storing the transformed PDF data in an RDS instance would require creating a table with columns to hold the text data, which can be inefficient and resource-intensive for large amounts of text. For a RAG chatbot, the primary requirement is to efficiently search and retrieve relevant information from PDF documents based on natural language queries. This is better achieved using specialized services like Amazon OpenSearch Service (vector database) or Amazon Bedrock (knowledge base)
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Comprehend to extract entities and key phrases from the PDF files and storing them in an Amazon DynamoDB table** is incorrect. While Comprehend can extract relevant information from PDF files, storing only entities and key phrases in DynamoDB may not provide enough context for the RAG chatbot to generate accurate and relevant responses.
    

A company has deployed a machine learning model using Amazon SageMaker AI with TensorFlow using SageMaker AI framework estimators. The model suggests personalized product recommendations to users based on their browsing history and purchase behavior. The company wants to continuously monitor the model’s performance and quality in production.

Which of the following monitoring options is the most suitable for their use case?

- **Continuous monitoring with a real-time endpoint.**
    
    With Amazon SageMaker Studio, you can visualize the results of monitoring a real-time endpoint. In SageMaker Studio, you can view the details of any monitoring job run and create charts that display the baseline and captured values for any metric calculated by the monitoring job.
    
    ![sagemaker-monitoring-real-time-endpoint](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/sagemaker-monitoring-real-time-endpoint-08-27-24.png)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Continuous monitoring with a real-time endpoint.** This option involves monitoring the model’s performance while it’s actively serving real-time predictions. It is suitable for use cases where low-latency responses are essential, such as recommendation systems. Real-time monitoring allows immediate detection of drift or anomalies.
    
    The option that says: **Continuous monitoring with a batch transform job** is incorrect. This option can periodically run batch transform jobs to analyze historical recommendation data and capture drift and quality metrics over time. However, it lacks real-time insights. It is primarily useful for long-term monitoring and trend analysis.
    
    The option that says: **On-schedule monitoring for asynchronous batch transform jobs** is incorrect. This option involves scheduling monitoring jobs at specific intervals. While it can detect drift and quality issues, it may not catch real-time anomalies.
    
    The option that says: **Monitoring SageMaker notebook instance metrics with CloudWatch** is incorrect. Although it is essential to monitor notebook instances, it only focuses on infrastructure metrics (CPU, memory) rather than model-specific performance.

- On-schedule monitoring for asynchronous batch transform jobs.
- Monitoring SageMaker notebook instance metrics with CloudWatch.

An international banking corporation is developing a machine learning model using Amazon SageMaker. The training dataset contains sensitive financial data and is stored in an Amazon S3 bucket in the us-east-1 region. SageMaker instances in the us-west-2 region are required for training. The company mandates that the data transfer between regions must be secure, ensuring no exposure to the public internet at any stage.

What solution best satisfies these requirements?

- **Ensure the direct internet access feature is disabled for the SageMaker instances. Establish an interface endpoint within the VPC to securely connect to Amazon S3.**
    
    **Amazon SageMaker** is a service that enables developers to build, train, and deploy large-scale machine-learning models. One key feature of SageMaker is its ability to securely connect to data stored in Amazon S3 via VPC endpoints. By configuring interface VPC endpoints, users can ensure that all communication between SageMaker instances and Amazon S3 remains within the AWS network, avoiding exposure to the public internet. This approach is crucial when handling sensitive data, such as financial information, ensuring that data is transferred securely between AWS regions.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-VPC-interface-endpoint-08-28-2024.png)
    
    Interface VPC endpoints allow SageMaker to connect to other AWS services without needing an internet gateway, NAT device, or VPN connection. This makes the data transfer process more secure and reduces the attack surface, as traffic between the services remains private. In scenarios where data needs to be transferred across regions, an interface VPC endpoint ensures that the connection is private and secure, protecting sensitive data from potential threats. Organizations can meet security and compliance requirements by restricting direct internet access and using VPC endpoints.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Ensure the direct internet access feature is disabled for the SageMaker instances. Establish an interface endpoint within the VPC to securely connect to Amazon S3.**
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Transfer Family to securely transfer the data between the regions using SFTP** is incorrect. This approach only provides secure file transfer protocols such as SFTP, FTPS, and FTP for transferring data. While it offers secure file transfers, it is not designed to address the specific requirement of ensuring secure data transfer between SageMaker instances and S3 without exposing the data to the public internet. Furthermore, the AWS Transfer Family does not intend to integrate directly with SageMaker for training across regions.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon S3 Cross-Region Replication to securely replicate data to the us-west-2 region, then train the model in that region** is incorrect. This solution would unnecessarily duplicate the dataset, increasing costs, storage usage, and replication time. The requirement is to securely access the data in real-time from SageMaker without public internet exposure, not to replicate it, making CRR an inefficient approach.
    
    The option that says: **Configure SageMaker instances to operate without direct internet access. Set up a gateway endpoint within the VPC for secure communication with Amazon S3** is incorrect. This approach is primarily used to access AWS services such as Amazon S3 and DynamoDB within the same region. Since the scenario requires secure data transfer between different regions, a gateway VPC endpoint would not meet the cross-region communication requirement since it does not support it.
    
- Use AWS Transfer Family to securely transfer the data between the regions using SFTP.
- Use Amazon S3 Cross-Region Replication to securely replicate data to the us-west-2 region, then train the model in that region.
- Configure SageMaker instances to operate without direct internet access. Set up a gateway endpoint within the VPC for secure communication with Amazon S3.

A company has developed a deep learning model for image classification. Which AWS service or feature would determine the optimal instance type and configuration for deploying the model?

- Amazon SageMaker Automatic Scaling
- **Amazon SageMaker Inference Recommender**
    
    Amazon SageMaker AI is a comprehensive, fully managed service designed to facilitate every step of the machine learning lifecycle, including model building, training, and deployment. It offers multiple deployment options such as real-time inference endpoints, batch transform jobs, and serverless inference to cater to different use cases. The performance and cost of these options can vary significantly depending on the instance type chosen. Therefore, selecting the appropriate instance type is crucial for optimizing both performance and cost-effectiveness for your specific machine learning needs.

    is a feature that helps determine the optimal instance type and configuration for deploying a machine learning model based on performance requirements and cost considerations. It analyzes the model and provides recommendations for the most cost-effective instance type that meets the specified latency and throughput targets.

However, this feature does not help in determining the optimal instance type and configuration for deploying a single model.

- Amazon SageMaker Multi-Model Endpoints
