A manufacturing company has developed a recommendation system using Amazon SageMaker AI. However, the dataset used for training is highly imbalanced, and the company is concerned about potential data drift affecting its model’s performance. The company wants an automated approach to evaluating and mitigating bias in its recommendations.

What is the recommended approach to achieve this?

- Utilize Amazon SageMaker Neo to optimize model performance and reduce data drift.
- **Use Amazon SageMaker Clarify to detect bias and monitor data drift automatically.**
    
    **Amazon SageMaker Clarify** is a tool that helps organizations identify and mitigate bias in their machine-learning models. It offers capabilities for bias detection during the data preparation stage, after model training, and throughout the model’s deployment. SageMaker Clarify works by analyzing both the training data and the model’s predictions to uncover any potential bias that could lead to unfair outcomes. It provides detailed reports highlighting areas of concern and suggests mitigation strategies to ensure the fairness and accuracy of the model. Additionally, Clarify can be integrated into the model development workflow to continuously monitor for bias, helping maintain model integrity over time.
    
    ![data-drift](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-data-drift-08-15-2024.png)
    
    Furthermore, SageMaker Clarify also includes functionality for monitoring data drift, which is critical in maintaining model performance as real-world data evolves. Data drift occurs when the distribution of incoming data changes over time, potentially leading to model degradation. By continuously analyzing incoming data and comparing it with the training data, SageMaker Clarify can detect drift and alert data scientists to take corrective action, such as retraining the model. This automated approach to bias detection and data drift monitoring ensures that models remain fair, accurate, and effective in dynamic environments, making SageMaker Clarify an essential tool for responsible AI development.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use Amazon SageMaker Clarify to detect bias and monitor data drift automatically.**
    
    The option that says: **Utilize Amazon SageMaker Neo to optimize model performance and reduce data drift** is incorrect because this service is primarily designed for optimizing machine learning models to run more efficiently on various hardware platforms, such as CPUs, GPUs, and edge devices. It compiles models into an optimized format that can be deployed on multiple hardware architectures with lower latency and better performance. SageMaker Neo does not address data drift or bias detection, which are the primary concerns in this scenario.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth to label the data more accurately and prevent data drift** is incorrect. This option only focuses on creating high-quality labeled datasets using human annotators and automated data labeling techniques. SageMaker Ground Truth does not provide features for detecting or mitigating data drift or bias in models. It is not designed to monitor model performance after deployment or to handle issues related to imbalanced datasets.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor to detect the model when performance drops** is incorrect. While SageMaker Model Monitor can track the quality of deployed models and detect data drift, it does not offer comprehensive bias detection and mitigation capabilities. Model Monitor can alert when there is a degradation in model performance, but it does not specifically focus on bias detection or provide automated mitigation strategies.
    
- Use Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth to label the data more accurately and prevent data drift.
- Utilize Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor to detect the model when performance drops.

A company is developing a sentiment analysis model that processes text data from customer reviews. The company collects and stores these reviews in Amazon S3 buckets. The data preprocessing pipeline involves text cleaning, tokenization, and embedding generation using machine learning frameworks such as TensorFlow and Hugging Face’s Transformers. The processed data is then used for training and fine-tuning the model.

Which set of actions will accomplish these tasks with the MINIMUM management effort? (Select THREE.)

- Use AWS Glue to preprocess and clean the text data and store the output in Amazon S3.
- **Create a SageMaker Processing Job to preprocess the text data, generate embeddings, and store the output in Amazon S3.**
- Utilize AWS Batch to manage and execute the SageMaker Processing Jobs for data preprocessing.
- **Use Amazon S3 Event Notifications to trigger an AWS Step Functions workflow for data preprocessing.**
- Deploy a custom Amazon ECS cluster on AWS Fargate to run the preprocessing scripts.
- **Create an AWS Lambda function to trigger a SageMaker Processing Job when new data is uploaded to the S3 bucket.**
- **Evaluation**
    
    Amazon SageMaker Processing Job is a fully managed service that simplifies the process of running data processing and model evaluation jobs. By leveraging SageMaker Processing Jobs, the entire pipeline from preprocessing to embedding generation can be handled with minimal management effort.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-s3-eventNotif-StepFx-sagemaker-diagram-29-08-2024.png)
    
    **SageMaker Processing Jobs** are designed to handle tasks such as data preprocessing and transformation, which align with the requirements of this scenario. They offer built-in support for TensorFlow and Hugging Face’s Transformers and can directly interact with data stored in Amazon S3.
    
    **AWS Lambda** can be used to automate the initiation of SageMaker Processing Jobs based on specific events, such as the upload of new data to S3. This ensures that preprocessing starts automatically without manual intervention.
    
    **Amazon S3 Event Notifications** can trigger workflows or functions in response to events in an S3 bucket. Using Lambda in combination with SageMaker Processing provides a streamlined approach to handling data preprocessing tasks.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Create an AWS Lambda function to trigger a SageMaker Processing Job when new data is uploaded to the S3 bucket.**
    
    **– Create a SageMaker Processing Job to preprocess the text data, generate embeddings, and store the output in Amazon S3.**
    
    **– Use Amazon S3 Event Notifications to trigger an AWS Step Functions workflow for data preprocessing.**
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Glue to preprocess and clean the text data and store the output in Amazon S3** is incorrect because AWS Glue is primarily designed for ETL tasks, requiring setup like defining schemas and creating crawlers, which adds unnecessary management overhead. SageMaker Processing Jobs are better suited for machine learning workflows, offering straightforward integration with ML libraries like TensorFlow and Hugging Face for preprocessing and embedding generation.
    
    The option that says: **Deploy a custom Amazon ECS cluster on AWS Fargate to run the preprocessing scripts** is incorrect. While Amazon Elastic Container Service (ECS) and AWS Fargate can be used primarily to run custom containers, they are not the most suitable choices for data preprocessing tasks in this scenario. SageMaker Processing Jobs and AWS Lambda functions are more specialized and managed services for data preprocessing and machine learning workloads.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize AWS Batch to manage and execute the SageMaker Processing Jobs for data preprocessing** is incorrect. AWS Batch is a batch computing service primarily used for running batch jobs on AWS resources, such as EC2 instances or ECS clusters. While it can be used to run SageMaker Processing Jobs, it is not necessary in this scenario, as SageMaker Processing Jobs can be managed and executed directly without the need for AWS Batch.
    

A retail company runs a machine learning model on a scheduled basis to predict sales, with data processing taking 45 minutes. It only needs to run once daily, and minimizing costs is the priority.

What is the MOST cost-effective solution for deploying this model?

- **Implement the model for processing using an Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform job.**
    
    Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform is a feature that enables you to run inference on large datasets without needing to maintain a persistent endpoint. This service is ideal for scenarios where you need to process data in batches, especially when the task can be scheduled and does not require immediate real-time responses. Batch Transform allows you to use pre-trained models for batch inference, which can handle processing jobs that take anywhere from a few minutes to several hours or even days, making it suitable for long-running jobs like daily sales predictions.
    
    ![Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/TD-Amazon-SageMaker-Batch-Tranform-jobs_30Aug2024.png)
    
    In this scenario, the retail company needs to run a model once daily with a processing time of 45 minutes. Since the model does not require real-time interaction and runs on a predictable schedule, using Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform is the most cost-effective approach. It allows the company to avoid the costs associated with keeping an endpoint live 24/7, making it the best choice for minimizing expenses while still meeting the operational requirements.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Implement the model for processing using an Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform job.**
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon EMR to process the data and invoke the model** is incorrect because it is primarily designed for big data processing using distributed frameworks like Hadoop and Spark. While EMR can process large volumes of data, it’s not optimized for the specific use case of scheduled model inference, and maintaining an EMR cluster would likely incur higher costs than using SageMaker’s managed batch transform service.
    
    The option that says: **Deploy the model using Amazon Bedrock for periodic inference** is incorrect because this approach is only focused on deploying and scaling foundational models for generative AI applications, rather than traditional batch processing of machine learning models. Using Bedrock for this scenario would add unnecessary complexity and cost, as it is not tailored for the specific requirement of scheduled inference tasks.
    
    The option that says: **Deploy the model using an Amazon SageMaker serverless endpoint** is incorrect because SageMaker Serverless Inference is typically designed for workloads with unpredictable traffic that require low latency, with a maximum processing time of 60 seconds. Since the model in this scenario requires 45 minutes to process data, serverless endpoints are not suitable, making this option impractical and less cost-effective.
    
- Deploy the model using an Amazon SageMaker serverless endpoint.
- Use Amazon EMR to process the data and invoke the model.
- Deploy the model using Amazon Bedrock for periodic inference.

A wildlife conservation organization uses drones to monitor endangered animals in a protected reserve area. It needs to count individual animals accurately using drone imagery. The organization is seeking advice on the best Amazon SageMaker AI machine-learning model for detecting and counting animals in the images.

Which machine learning built-in algorithm is most suitable for this task?

- **Object detection algorithm**
    
    The **object detection algorithm** in Amazon SageMaker is specifically designed to identify and locate multiple objects within an image. It draws bounding boxes around detected objects and classifies them into different categories, making it very useful for tasks where you need to recognize what is in the image and determine the exact location of each object. This algorithm is well-suited for scenarios that require counting specific items, such as animals, in drone imagery, as it can distinguish between individual objects even in complex scenes.
    
    ![object-detection](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-object-detection-08-30-2024.png)
    
    Amazon SageMaker AI’s object detection algorithm uses deep learning techniques, such as convolutional neural networks (CNNs), to achieve high accuracy in identifying and localizing objects. It is pre-trained on large datasets and can be fine-tuned with your data to meet specific needs. This flexibility makes it ideal for applications like wildlife monitoring, where precise counting and detection are critical. By using this algorithm, organizations can automate the monitoring of large areas, significantly improving the efficiency and accuracy of their conservation efforts.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Object detection algorithm.**
    
    The option that says: **Semantic segmentation algorithm** is incorrect because this algorithm only classifies each pixel in an image into different categories, providing a detailed map of where different objects or materials are located within the image. This algorithm is typically used in fields such as autonomous vehicles to identify road signs and medical imaging to segment anatomical structures.
    
    The option that says: **Image classification algorithm** is incorrect. This option simply assigns a single label to an entire image, categorizing it based on the predominant features. Additionally, it works well for tasks like sorting images into broad categories but cannot identify or count multiple objects within a single image.
    
    The option that says: **Object2Vec algorithm** is incorrect because it is a general-purpose algorithm primarily used for learning vector representations of objects, which can then be used for tasks like similarity search, recommendation, or clustering. Therefore, this algorithm is not designed for image analysis or object detection.
    
- Image classification algorithm
- Object2Vec algorithm
- Semantic segmentation algorithm

A media analytics company is working on a project that analyzes video and image files stored in an Amazon S3 bucket. The company has partnered with an external video processing startup to improve its machine learning (ML) models. The startup uses its own AWS account but requires access to the media analytics company’s S3 bucket to retrieve and process the video files. The startup needs both programmatic and AWS Management Console access to the bucket.

Which solution will meet these requirements most securely?

- Use Amazon S3 Access Points to allow the startup access to the S3 bucket.
- Create a bucket policy in the startup's AWS account, granting access to the S3 bucket.
- **Create an IAM role in the media analytics company's AWS account, allowing the startup to assume the role and access the S3 bucket.**
    
    An **IAM role** is an AWS identity with specific permissions intended to be assumed by entities (users, applications, services). IAM roles do not have long-term credentials. Instead, they are designed to provide temporary security credentials that expire after a short period, typically ranging from a few minutes to a maximum of 12 hours. When you create an IAM role, you can attach one or more identity-based policies. These policies define what actions the role can perform when it is assumed.
    
    A **cross-account IAM role** is an IAM role that can be assumed by users or services in another AWS account. This allows for secure access and sharing of resources across AWS accounts.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-IAM-cross-account-30-08-2024.png)
    
    By creating an IAM role in the media analytics company’s account and granting the necessary permissions to access the S3 bucket, the video processing startup can assume this role and securely access the bucket. This approach follows the principle of least privilege and allows for granular access control.
    
    The process involves:
    
    1. Creating an IAM role in the media analytics company’s account with the required permissions to access the S3 bucket.
    2. Granting the video processing startup’s AWS account the ability to assume this role using the `sts:AssumeRole` permission.
    3. The video processing startup can then assume the role and access the S3 bucket using temporary security credentials provided by AWS Security Token Service (STS).
    
    This solution ensures that the video processing startup does not have permanent access to the media analytics company’s AWS resources and can only access the S3 bucket through the assumed role.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create an IAM role in the media analytics company’s AWS account, allowing the startup to assume the role and access the S3 bucket.**
    
    The option that says: **Create a bucket policy in the startup’s AWS account, granting access to the S3 bucket** is incorrect because bucket policies are defined only in the bucket owner’s account, not the account accessing the bucket. Creating a bucket policy in the startup’s account would not grant them access to the media analytics company’s bucket.
    
    The option that says: **Enable S3 Block Public Access in the media analytics company’s AWS account** is incorrect. While enabling S3 Block Public Access is a recommended security practice, it does not directly address the requirement of granting access to the video processing startup’s AWS account. This option alone would not allow the startup to access the S3 bucket.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon S3 Access Points to allow the startup access to the S3 bucket** is incorrect. S3 Access Points typically provide a network path to access S3 buckets but do not handle access control or authentication. Access Points must be used with other access management mechanisms, such as IAM roles or bucket policies. Additionally, they do not inherently provide a mechanism for granting programmatic and AWS Management Console access across different AWS accounts. IAM roles with cross-account access are still required for this scenario.
    
- Enable S3 Block Public Access in the media analytics company's AWS account.

An ML engineer oversees a machine learning model deployed on Amazon SageMaker AI. To handle varying workloads, the engineer decided to implement automatic scaling. They configured a target tracking scaling policy to adjust the number of instances based on the average CPU utilization. However, the engineer noticed that the scaling actions were occurring too frequently, leading to instability in the application.

Which of the following options must be configured to resolve the issue?

- Use a different CloudWatch metric for scaling.
- **Set a cooldown period between scaling actions.**
    
    A hosted model in Amazon SageMaker AI can be set up to automatically scale (auto scale) based on changes in your workload. When the workload increases, auto-scaling adds more instances to handle the increased demand. Conversely, auto-scaling removes unnecessary instances when the workload decreases to avoid paying for unused provisioned instances.
    
    ![Scaling Cooldown Period](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/scaling-cooldown-period-08-30-24.png)
    
    A **cooldown period** is a setting that determines the duration to wait after a scaling activity finishes before initiating another scaling activity. By implementing a cooldown period, you can avoid excessive scaling, giving the application time to stabilize before making further adjustments. This helps minimize instability resulting from rapid scaling and allows the system sufficient time to adapt to the new capacity.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Set a cooldown period between scaling actions.**
    
    The option that says: **Increase the target value for CPU utilization** is incorrect. Increasing the target value for CPU utilization typically means that the scaling policy will aim for a higher average CPU usage before triggering scaling actions. While this might reduce the frequency of scaling actions, it doesn’t directly address the issue of frequent scaling. It could also lead to under-provisioning, where your instances are overworked before scaling up, potentially impacting performance.
    
    The option that says: **Decrease the minimum number of instances** is incorrect. Decreasing the minimum number of instances primarily reduces the baseline capacity of your application. This might lead to more frequent scaling actions as the system initially tries to meet the demand with fewer resources.
    
    The option that says: **Use a different CloudWatch metric for scaling** is incorrect because using a different CloudWatch metric for scaling might help if the current metric is not a good indicator of the load on your application. However, this does not directly address the issue of frequent scaling actions. The problem of rapid scaling is more about the timing and frequency of scaling actions rather than the specific metric being used. Changing the metric might not solve the underlying issue of instability caused by frequent scaling.
    
- Increase the target value for CPU utilization.
- Decrease the minimum number of instances.

A manufacturing company wants to develop a machine learning (ML) model for predicting equipment failures and optimizing maintenance schedules. The company has a team of data scientists who have developed custom Python scripts for data preprocessing, feature engineering, and model training. The company wants to leverage AWS services to build, train, and deploy its ML models in a scalable and automated manner.

Which approach should the company take to incorporate its custom scripts into the SageMaker AI environment?

- Create a custom AWS Lambda function with the Python scripts and invoke it from SageMaker AI.
- Store the custom Python scripts in an Amazon S3 bucket and use them directly in SageMaker AI.
- **Create a custom Docker container with the required Python dependencies, push it to the Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR), and use it as a processing container in SageMaker AI**
    
    **Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR)** is a fully managed container registry offering high-performance hosting, so you can reliably deploy application images and artifacts anywhere.
    
    ![ECR-sagemaker](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-ECR-sagemaker-02-09-2024.png)
    
    Amazon SageMaker AI supports the use of custom Docker containers to run code that is tailored to specific requirements, such as custom libraries, dependencies, or Python scripts developed by the company. By creating a Docker container, pushing it to Amazon Elastic Container Registry (ECR), and using it as a processing or training container in SageMaker AI, the company can ensure that the environment is configured with the exact dependencies needed for its custom Python scripts.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create a custom Docker container with the required Python dependencies, push it to the Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR), and use it as a processing container in SageMaker AI.**
    
    The option that says: **Create a custom AWS Lambda function with the Python scripts and invoke it from SageMaker AI** is incorrect because AWS Lambda is designed for running short-lived, stateless functions and is not typically used for complex ML workflows or training models.
    
    The option that says: **Create a container image for Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS)** is incorrect. While Amazon EKS is a managed Kubernetes service, it is not the recommended approach for integrating custom scripts with SageMaker. SageMaker is primarily designed to work with Docker containers, and using EKS for this purpose would introduce unnecessary complexity.
    
    The option that says: **Store the custom Python scripts in an Amazon S3 bucket and use them directly in SageMaker AI** is incorrect because Amazon S3 is an object storage service not designed to run executable code directly. The custom Python scripts must be packaged and executed within a container or computing environment.
    
- Create a container image for Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS).

A manufacturing company wants to develop a machine learning (ML) model for predicting equipment failures and optimizing maintenance schedules. The company has a team of data scientists who have developed custom Python scripts for data preprocessing, feature engineering, and model training. The company wants to leverage AWS services to build, train, and deploy its ML models in a scalable and automated manner.

Which AWS service should the company use to orchestrate continuous integration and continuous deployment (CI/CD) pipeline?

- AWS Step Functions
- AWS CodeBuild
- **AWS CodePipeline**
    
    **AWS CodePipeline** is a fully managed continuous delivery service that helps you automate your release pipelines for fast and reliable application and infrastructure updates. CodePipeline automates your release process’s build, test, and deploy phases every time there is a code change based on the release model you define.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-codepipeline-19-Aug-2024.png)
    
    AWS CodePipeline is designed to orchestrate the entire CI/CD pipeline, including building, testing, and deploying code changes across multiple AWS services. It can integrate with AWS CodeBuild for building and testing the custom Python scripts, AWS SageMaker for training and evaluating the ML models, and AWS CodeDeploy or AWS SageMaker for deploying the trained models to production environments.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **AWS CodePipeline.**
    
    The option that says **AWS CodeBuild** is incorrect because it is a fully managed continuous integration service that can compile source code, run tests, and produce software packages ready to deploy. While it can be used as part of a CI/CD pipeline, it is not the primary service for orchestrating the entire pipeline.
    
    The option that says: **AWS CodeDeploy** is incorrect because it’s only used for automating software deployments to various compute services such as EC2, Lambda, and on-premises servers. It is focused on the deployment phase, not the orchestration of the entire CI/CD pipeline.
    
    The option that says: **AWS Step Functions** is incorrect. This service is used to coordinate workflows, including complex, long-running processes. However, it is not specialized for CI/CD pipeline orchestration. CodePipeline is a service built specifically for CI/CD automation.
    
- AWS CodeDeploy

A media streaming platform uses an Amazon SageMaker AI endpoint to provide personalized content suggestions to users in real-time. The platform expects a significant increase in traffic during a major global sports event. An ML engineer must verify that the model can handle the surge in endpoint invocations while minimizing latency.

What is the most effective solution to optimize performance and scalability in this scenario?

- Deploy the SageMaker AI endpoint in a multi-model endpoint configuration.
- **Set up auto-scaling on the SageMaker AI model endpoint to dynamically adjust capacity based on traffic demands.**
    
    Auto-scaling on an Amazon SageMaker model AI endpoint allows the system to automatically adjust the number of instances handling the endpoint’s requests based on the current traffic load. When traffic increases, more instances are added to manage the demand, and when traffic decreases, the system reduces the number of instances to save resources. This automatic adjustment ensures that the endpoint can handle varying levels of traffic efficiently, providing a seamless experience for users without requiring manual adjustments.
    
    ![Amazon_SageMaker_Endpoint](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/TD-Amazon_SageMaker_Endpoint_2Sept2024.png)
    
    For the media streaming platform anticipating a huge increase in users during a major sports event, auto-scaling is important to ensure the system can manage the sudden surge in requests. As more people tune in and seek personalized content recommendations, the demand for the SageMaker endpoint will spike. With auto-scaling, the platform can automatically expand its capacity to meet this demand, ensuring that users continue to receive fast and accurate recommendations without delays. This makes auto-scaling the most effective solution for maintaining performance and scalability during high-traffic events.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Set up auto-scaling on the SageMaker AI model endpoint to dynamically adjust capacity based on traffic demands.**
    
    The option that says: **Deploy the SageMaker AI endpoint in a multi-model endpoint configuration** is incorrect because it is primarily used to serve multiple models from a single endpoint, which is beneficial for saving costs when running many models with low traffic. However, it is not the best solution for handling a significant surge in traffic for a single model, as it doesn’t directly address the need for scaling to meet high demand or minimizing latency during traffic spikes.
    
    The option that says: **Implement SageMaker AI endpoint variants to distribute traffic across different model versions** is incorrect because this approach is simply used for A/B testing or gradually rolling out new model versions, which is useful for model experimentation and validation.
    
    The option that says: **Leverage SageMaker Neo to enhance the efficiency of the ML model endpoint for inference** is incorrect because SageMaker Neo is only focused on optimizing models to run efficiently on specific hardware, which can reduce inference latency and improve throughput. While this can enhance performance, it doesn’t inherently scale the system to handle increased traffic. Therefore, it does not address the requirement to dynamically adjust capacity in response to the expected surge in traffic during the global sports event.
    
- Implement SageMaker AI endpoint variants to distribute traffic across different model versions.
- Leverage SageMaker Neo to enhance the efficiency of the ML model endpoint for inference.

A data scientist is planning to work on a machine learning project using a fully managed service to automate and oversee the entire workflow. The scientist must ensure smooth integration with other AWS services from data preparation to model deployment.

Which of the following options is the most suitable for this use case?

- Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow (Amazon MWAA)
- AWS Step Functions
- **Amazon SageMaker Pipelines**
    
    **Amazon SageMaker Pipelines** is a serverless workflow orchestration service designed specifically for MLOps and LLMOps automation. You can effortlessly create, execute, and monitor consistent end-to-end ML workflows using an intuitive drag-and-drop UI or the Python SDK. Amazon SageMaker Pipelines is capable of scaling to handle tens of thousands of concurrent ML workflows in production.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/sagemaker-data-pipeline-090224.png)
    
    Amazon SageMaker Pipelines automates and manages the entire process, from data preparation to model deployment. It seamlessly integrates with all SageMaker features and other AWS services to automate data processing, model training, fine-tuning, evaluation, deployment, and monitoring jobs. You can incorporate SageMaker features into your Pipelines and navigate through them using deep links to create, monitor, and debug your ML workflows at scale.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Amazon SageMaker Pipelines.**
    
    The option that says: **AWS Glue** is incorrect because, while it is a fully managed ETL service primarily useful for data preparation and transformation, it does not cover the complete machine learning workflow, including model training and deployment.

While it can manage machine learning workflows, it requires more manual setup. It does not offer the same level of integration and automation for machine learning tasks as SageMaker Pipelines.
    
    The option that says: **Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow (Amazon MWAA)** is incorrect because Amazon MWAA is a managed service for Apache Airflow, which is an open-source tool to programmatically author, schedule, and monitor workflows. It is not typically designed for machine learning workflows and requires more manual configuration compared to SageMaker Pipelines.
    
- AWS Glue

A data scientist is working on a machine learning project that requires extensive training in models. The goal is to minimize the cost of training while ensuring that the models are trained effectively.

Which of the following options is the most cost-effective way to train the models in Amazon SageMaker AI?

- Run the models in an Amazon EMR cluster.
- **Use Managed Spot Training in Amazon SageMaker AI.**
    
    Amazon SageMaker AI simplifies the process of training machine learning models by utilizing managed Amazon EC2 Spot instances. Managed spot training can reduce the cost of training models by up to 90% compared to using on-demand instances. Additionally, SageMaker handles any interruptions in Spot instances on your behalf.
    
    With managed spot training, Amazon EC2 Spot instances are used to execute training jobs instead of on-demand instances. You have the flexibility to specify which training jobs utilize spot instances and set a stopping condition to determine how long SageMaker should wait for a job to run using Amazon EC2 Spot instances. Metrics and logs generated during training runs are accessible in CloudWatch.
    
    ![sagemaker-managed-spot-training](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/sagemaker-managed-spot-training-090224.png)
    
    In order to use managed spot training, you need to create a training job. Set `EnableManagedSpotTraining` to `True` and specify the `MaxWaitTimeInSeconds`. `MaxWaitTimeInSeconds` must be larger than `MaxRuntimeInSeconds`.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use Managed Spot Training in Amazon SageMaker AI.**
    
    The option that says: **Run the models in an Amazon EMR cluster** is incorrect because Amazon EMR (Elastic MapReduce) is primarily designed for big data processing using frameworks like Apache Hadoop and Apache Spark. While it can be used for machine learning tasks, it is not specifically optimized for training machine learning models in the same way that SageMaker is. Additionally, EMR clusters can be more expensive and complex to manage compared to SageMaker’s managed services.
    
    The option that says: **Use Reserved Instances for training in Amazon SageMaker AI** is incorrect. Reserved Instances provide a discount compared to On-Demand Instances. It typically requires a commitment to use the instances for a one- or three-year term. While this can be cost-effective in the long term, this feature is not supported for SageMaker training.
    
    The option that says: **Use On-Demand Instances for training in Amazon SageMaker AI** is incorrect because On-Demand Instances are more expensive than Managed Spot Training. On-demand instances charge you for computing capacity by the hour or second with no long-term commitments, leading to higher costs.
    
- Use Reserved Instances for training in Amazon SageMaker AI.
- Use On-Demand Instances for training in Amazon SageMaker AI.

A hospital is implementing a deep learning model that predicts patient readmissions based on discharge data and medical history. To deliver timely alerts to healthcare staff, the hospital must select an optimal Amazon EC2 instance type for hosting the model during inference, focusing on performance and reliability.

Which Amazon EC2 instance type is best for supporting the hospital’s model?

- General purpose group of ML instances
- High-performance group of ML instances
- Memory-optimized group of ML instances
- **Accelerated computing group of ML instances**
    
    **Amazon EC2** provides a diverse range of instance types tailored for different workloads. Each instance type belongs to one or more families, categorized by key resource configurations like compute power, memory capacity, storage options, and network performance.
    
    **– General-purpose instances** are created to offer a balanced combination of compute, memory, and networking resources. They are suitable for various applications, such as web servers, development environments, and databases that need balanced performance. The T, M, and A instance families fall into this category and provide adaptable compute power.
    
    **– Compute-optimized instances** are perfect for compute-bound applications that require high-performance processing, such as high-performance web servers, scientific modeling, and gaming servers. The C instance family falls under this category, offering greater compute power relative to memory.
    
    **– Memory-optimized instances** are specifically built for workloads that need substantial memory compared to computational power. This is ideal for in-memory databases, big data analytics, and high-performance computing (HPC) workloads. The R, X, and Z instance families offer a high memory-to-CPU ratio to cater to these requirements.
    
    **– Accelerated computing instances** feature GPUs or FPGAs (Field Programmable Gate Arrays) to accelerate graphics rendering, machine learning, and data-parallel computations. These instances, such as the P and G families, offer the best performance for tasks like deep learning inference and model training.
    
    **– Storage-optimized instances** are specifically designed for workloads that demand high disk throughput and low-latency storage. These instances offer rapid local storage for large datasets and are well-suited for big data workloads, NoSQL databases, and distributed file systems. Examples of storage-optimized instances include the I and D families.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td_instance_types_09_26_24.png)
    
    Each instance type within these families offers varying CPU, memory, storage, and network performance specifications. By selecting the right instance type, users can optimize their application performance and control costs based on their specific workload requirements.
    
    The *Accelerated computing group of ML instances*, such as P3 instances, provides GPU-based instances that are optimal for tasks requiring significant computational power, like deep learning inference. These instances use hardware accelerators, which offer enhanced processing speed and are ideal for machine learning (ML) models.
    
    Since the requirement is to support a deep learning model, the accelerated computing instances with GPU support are the most suitable. These instances can handle the computational requirements of deep learning inference, ensuring the model delivers predictions quickly and efficiently.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Accelerated computing group of ML instances.**
    
    The option that says: **High-performance group of ML instances** is incorrect because it focuses on delivering high compute power, and typically for tasks that require heavy CPU usage like scientific modeling, web-scale applications, or batch processing. However, these instances do not have the specialized hardware accelerators (such as GPUs or FPGAs) needed for machine learning inference tasks, which benefit from low latency and high throughput. ML inference, especially with deep learning models, relies heavily on GPUs for optimal performance.
    
    The option that says: **Memory-optimized group of ML instances** is incorrect because it is only suited for applications requiring large memory capacity, such as in-memory databases or big data analytics. While memory is important, machine learning inference workloads are typically not bound by memory constraints but by computational and acceleration requirements, particularly when real-time or near-real-time predictions are needed.
    
    The option that says: **General purpose group of ML instances** is incorrect because it provides a balanced mix of compute, memory, and networking resources. They are versatile for a broad range of applications but do not offer the level of performance needed for ML inference. General purpose instances lack specialized hardware for acceleration, such as GPUs, which are crucial for optimizing inference tasks in a machine learning model. For a hospital requiring timely alerts based on model predictions, the latency and performance offered by general-purpose instances would be insufficient compared to accelerated computing instances.
    

A data science team manages a machine learning model that uses an Amazon SageMaker AI endpoint for real-time predictions. The team is concerned about data drift and wants to set up a monitoring system that compares incoming data to the original training data stored in an S3 bucket. The team needs to establish a baseline of the training data for effective drift detection.

Which approach should the team take to establish this baseline and enable continuous monitoring for data drift in the deployed model?

- Use Amazon SageMaker Clarify to analyze the model's predictions on the inference data and generate a baseline from the model's explanations.
- Use Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler to preprocess and analyze the training data and create a baseline from the processed data.
- Use Amazon SageMaker Model Dashboard to visualize the training data and manually set a baseline for comparison.
- **Use Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor to analyze the training data and generate a baseline of statistics and constraints**
    
    In machine learning, data drift refers to the phenomenon where the distribution of the input data changes over time, potentially leading to degraded model performance. Data drift can occur for various reasons, such as changes in the underlying data-generating process, seasonal patterns, or external factors affecting the data. Detecting and addressing data drift is crucial for maintaining the accuracy and reliability of deployed machine learning models.
    
    **Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor** is a capability within Amazon SageMaker that helps monitor machine learning models for data drift, model quality, bias, and other issues. It can automatically establish a baseline from the training data and continuously monitor the incoming data for deviations from this baseline. An example diagram below illustrates how SageMaker Model Monitor works in practice.
    
    ![model-monitoring-diagram](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-model-monitoring-diagram-03-09-2024.png)
    
    Initially, the training data is stored in S3. SageMaker Model Monitor processes this data to create a baseline of statistics and constraints stored in S3. The deployed model endpoint uses this baseline for comparison as it processes incoming inference data. Continuous monitoring is performed by SageMaker Model Monitor to detect any discrepancies or drift from the established baseline. Metrics related to data drift are sent to CloudWatch, where alerts can be configured. If drift is detected, the model update process can be initiated to maintain accuracy.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor to analyze the training data and generate a baseline of statistics and constraints.**
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon SageMaker Model Dashboard to visualize the training data and manually set a baseline for comparison** is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Model Dashboard is a tool primarily used for visualizing and monitoring the performance of machine learning models deployed on SageMaker endpoints. It cannot analyze training data and automatically generate a baseline for data drift detection.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler to preprocess and analyze the training data and create a baseline from the processed data** is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler is just a data preparation tool that helps you import, explore, transform, and analyze data for machine learning. While it can preprocess and analyze data, it does not have built-in capabilities for generating baselines specifically for data drift detection. Its primary purpose is data preparation, not model monitoring.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon SageMaker Clarify to analyze the model’s predictions on the inference data and generate a baseline from the model’s explanations** is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Clarify is typically used to detect bias and explain machine learning models. It analyzes the model’s predictions on inference data and explains them. However, based on the training data, it does not generate baselines for data drift detection. It focuses on model explainability and bias detection, not data drift monitoring.
    

A retail company has deployed a recommendation system using an ML model on an Amazon SageMaker AI endpoint. The goal is to maintain the accuracy of the model’s recommendations by monitoring the input data and model predictions for any anomalies. Additionally, they need to implement a solution that reduces operational overhead.

Which of the following options is the most suitable for this use case?

- Use AWS Glue to extract, transform, and load the input data and model predictions into a data warehouse for anomaly detection.
- **Deploy SageMaker Model Monitor to continuously monitor the input data and model predictions for anomalies.**
    
    Once you have implemented a model into your production environment, you can use Amazon SageMaker model monitor to continuously monitor the quality of your machine learning models in real time. Amazon SageMaker model monitor allows you to set up an automated alert system that triggers alerts when there are deviations in the model quality, such as data drift and anomalies. Amazon CloudWatch Logs collects log files to monitor the model status and sends notifications when the quality of your model exceeds certain thresholds that you have set. CloudWatch stores the log files in an Amazon S3 bucket that you specify.
    
    ![sagemaker-monitoring-real-time-endpoint](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/sagemaker-monitoring-real-time-endpoint-08-27-24.png)
    
    Using SageMaker Model Monitor, you can monitor input data and model predictions for anomalies, and it can automatically set up alerts for any deviations. This solution is highly integrated with SageMaker, reducing operational overhead by automating the monitoring process.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Deploy SageMaker Model Monitor to continuously monitor the input data and model predictions for anomalies.**
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Glue to extract, transform, and load the input data and model predictions into a data warehouse for anomaly detection** is incorrect because AWS Glue is primarily an ETL (Extract, Transform, Load) service. While it can be used to process and transform data, it is not designed for real-time monitoring of model predictions. Using AWS Glue for this purpose would require additional custom development and would not reduce operational overhead effectively.
    
    The option that says: **Deploy an Amazon EC2 instance that runs a custom script to monitor the input data and model predictions for anomalies** is incorrect. Deploying an Amazon EC2 instance to run a custom script for monitoring would involve significant manual setup and maintenance. This approach does not leverage the built-in capabilities of SageMaker and would increase operational overhead rather than reduce it.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon QuickSight to create dashboards that visualize the input data and model predictions for anomaly detection** is incorrect because Amazon QuickSight is a business intelligence service typically used to create visualizations and dashboards. While it can visualize data, it is not designed for real-time anomaly detection or monitoring of model predictions. This option would not provide the automated monitoring and alerting capabilities required for this use case.
    
- Deploy an Amazon EC2 instance that runs a custom script to monitor the input data and model predictions for anomalies.
- Use Amazon QuickSight to create dashboards that visualize the input data and model predictions for anomaly detection.

A retail company uses Amazon SageMaker AI to train machine learning models with data sourced from Amazon Redshift. The data requires preprocessing, including filtering, normalization, and feature engineering. The company needs to automate these steps and ensure the processed features are stored, versioned, and retained in the SageMaker Feature Store each month, all with minimal coding effort.

What is the most efficient way to achieve this with minimal coding effort? (Select TWO.)

- Utilize AWS Batch to load training data from Amazon Redshift, run all transformations, and store features in Feature Store.
- Manage Amazon Athena to query the data in Redshift, preprocess it, and store the features in SageMaker Feature Store
- Use a pre-configured AWS Glue for Spark job template to retrieve training data from Amazon Redshift, execute the required transformations, and save the output features in SageMaker Feature Store.
- **Leverage SageMaker Data Wrangler to import data from Amazon Redshift, apply transformations, and store the features in SageMaker Feature Store.**
- **Set up the data preprocessing as a SageMaker AI processing job and configure it to execute on a monthly schedule.**
- **Evaluation**
    
    **Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler** is a tool within Amazon SageMaker Studio that offers a complete solution for importing, preparing, transforming, and analyzing data. It helps simplify the data preprocessing and feature engineering process for machine learning, often without needing much coding. It also provides flexibility by allowing the addition of custom Python scripts and transformations if needed. Data Wrangler also offers seamless integration with Amazon Redshift and SageMaker Feature Store, ensuring that the processed features can be easily stored and versioned as required.
    
    **SageMaker AI Processing** allows you to handle tasks like data preprocessing, feature engineering, and model evaluation on SageMaker’s managed infrastructure. These tasks are carried out as processing jobs, which allow data scientists to run scripts and notebooks to prepare data for machine learning. SageMaker Processing provides the capability to schedule and run these jobs regularly (e.g., monthly) and is well-integrated with SageMaker Feature Store, making it easy to store the processed features for further use. This setup allows automation and scheduling of preprocessing tasks with minimal code.
    
    ![Amazon_SageMaker-Processing-Job](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/TD-Amazon_SageMaker-Processing-Job-3Sept2024.png)
    
    Using SageMaker Data Wrangler and SageMaker Processing is the most efficient approach to automating data preprocessing efficiently with minimal coding. SageMaker Data Wrangler simplifies the process of preparing and transforming data, making it easy to prepare data for machine learning without requiring significant coding. It also allows custom Python scripts when necessary. On the other hand, SageMaker Processing jobs simply facilitate the scheduling and execution of preprocessing tasks on SageMaker’s managed infrastructure. This will ensure the company’s data is regularly updated and available for model training. As a result, these services will streamline the data preparation and automation process.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Set up the data preprocessing as a SageMaker AI processing job and configure it to execute on a monthly schedule.**
    
    **– Leverage SageMaker Data Wrangler to import data from Amazon Redshift, apply transformations, and store the features in SageMaker Feature Store.**
    
    The option that says: **Utilize AWS Batch to load training data from Amazon Redshift, run all transformations, and store features in Feature Store** is incorrect. This option is primarily used for general batch computing workloads. While capable of data processing, it’s not optimized for ML preprocessing or SageMaker integration. Using Batch would require more custom coding and management compared to purpose-built ML data preparation tools like SageMaker Processing Jobs or Data Wrangler.
    
    The option that says: **Use a pre-configured AWS Glue for Spark job template to retrieve training data from Amazon Redshift, execute the required transformations, and save the output features in SageMaker Feature Store** is incorrect. While AWS Glue can handle ETL tasks, it typically requires more coding and configuration compared to SageMaker’s built-in tools, such as Data Wrangler. AWS Glue is more complex and doesn’t provide the same no-code experience, making it less efficient for the company’s requirements.
    
    The option that says: **Manage Amazon Athena to query the data in Redshift, preprocess it, and store the features in SageMaker Feature Store** is incorrect because this service is simply used for querying and analyzing data but is not optimized for feature engineering or seamless integration with SageMaker Feature Store. Athena’s purpose is for querying, and it lacks the advanced transformation capabilities needed for this scenario.
    

A financial company handles sensitive data across various regions. The company receives scanned copies of client identification documents and uses Amazon Textract to extract text from these documents. To comply with privacy regulations, the company must redact sensitive information, such as social security numbers (SSNs), from the extracted text before it can be used for further analysis.

Which approach will fulfill these requirements while minimizing operational complexity?

- Use Amazon Textract’s `AnalyzeDocument` operation to identify SSNs in the extracted text. Replace the SSNs with masking characters.
- Use AWS Glue to create a job that processes the extracted text, applying a custom Python script to detect and redact SSNs. Configure the job to run periodically as needed.
- **Use Amazon Comprehend to execute an asynchronous job for redacting PII (Personally Identifiable Information) entities. Configure the job to redact SSNs from the text.**
    
    **Amazon Comprehend** uses natural language processing (NLP) to extract insights about the content of documents. It develops insights by recognizing the entities, key phrases, language, sentiments, and other common elements in a document. Use Amazon Comprehend to create new products based on understanding the structure of documents.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-amazon-comprehend-async-job-11-09-2024.png)
    
    Amazon Comprehend provides native capabilities for detecting and redacting Personally Identifiable Information (PII), such as SSNs in text documents. This service minimizes operational complexity because it directly supports asynchronous processing of text and automatic PII detection and redaction without needing to implement custom logic or manage workflows.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use Amazon Comprehend to execute an asynchronous job for redacting PII (Personally Identifiable Information) entities. Configure the job to redact SSNs from the text.**
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Step Functions to orchestrate a workflow that invokes Amazon Textract for text extraction, then triggers a Lambda function to detect and redact SSNs in the text** is incorrect because while this approach is valid, it adds unnecessary complexity compared to using Amazon Comprehend, which has built-in PII detection.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Glue to create a job that processes the extracted text, applying a custom Python script to detect and redact SSNs. Configure the job to run periodically as needed** is incorrect because AWS Glue is primarily used for large-scale ETL (Extract, Transform, Load) tasks, and using it with custom scripts adds significant operational overhead compared to using a native service like Amazon Comprehend.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Textract’s `AnalyzeDocument` operation to identify SSNs in the extracted text. Replace the SSNs with masking characters** is incorrect because Amazon Textract is primarily designed for extracting structured data from documents, not for redacting sensitive data. Developing custom logic to mask PII would require more work
    
- Use AWS Step Functions to orchestrate a workflow that invokes Amazon Textract for text extraction, then triggers a Lambda function to detect and redact SSNs in the text.

An ML engineer has an Amazon SageMaker Processing job that requires access to a file named `data.csv`, which is stored in an Amazon S3 bucket. The job can access the S3 bucket, but it encounters a 403 error when trying to access the specific file.

Which of the following options is causing the 403 error?

- The S3 bucket policy explicitly denies access to the SageMaker AI Processing job execution role.
- The S3 bucket is encrypted with an AWS KMS key, and the SageMaker AI Processing job execution role does not have the necessary KMS permissions.
- The SageMaker Studio execution role does not have the `s3:GetObject`permission for the file `data.csv`.
- **The SageMaker AI Processing job execution role does not have the `s3:GetObject` permission for the file `data.csv`.**
- **Evaluation**
    
    Amazon SageMaker AI carries out operations on your behalf by utilizing other AWS services. To enable SageMaker to use these services and the associated resources, you need to grant it permissions through an AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) execution role.
    
    ![iam-policy-access-specific-files-s3](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/iam-policy-access-specific-files-s3-091124.png)
    
    The SageMaker AI Processing job requires specific permissions to access objects in an S3 bucket. If the execution role assigned to the job does not have the `s3:GetObject` permission for the file `data.csv`, it will encounter a 403 Forbidden error when trying to access the file. This permission is essential for reading the object from the S3 bucket.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **The SageMaker AI Processing job execution role does not have the `s3:GetObject` permission for the file `data.csv`.**
    
    The option that says: **The SageMaker Studio execution role does not have the `s3:GetObject` permission for the file `data.csv**` is incorrect because the SageMaker Studio execution role is different from the SageMaker AI Processing job execution role. The Studio execution role is primarily used for tasks within the SageMaker Studio environment, whereas the Processing job execution role is specifically for the processing job. The 403 error is related to the permissions of the Processing job execution role, not the Studio execution role.
    
    The option that says: **The S3 bucket policy explicitly denies access to the SageMaker AI Processing job execution role** is incorrect. While an explicit deny in the S3 bucket policy could cause a 403 error, this option is less likely because it would require a specific deny rule targeting the Processing job execution role. The more common issue is missing permissions rather than an explicit deny.
    
    The option that says: **The S3 bucket is encrypted with an AWS KMS key, and the SageMaker AI Processing job execution role does not have the necessary KMS permissions** is incorrect because if the S3 bucket is encrypted with a KMS key and the Processing job execution role does not have the necessary KMS permissions, it would typically result in a different error related to encryption, not a 403 Forbidden error. The 403 error is more directly related to missing `s3:GetObject` permissions.
    

A machine learning engineer is deploying a new version of a model in Amazon SageMaker AI. To minimize the risk of introducing issues, the engineer wants to use a deployment strategy that shifts a small portion of the traffic to the new model while the rest continues to be served by the old model.

Which deployment strategy in SageMaker AI must be chosen to achieve this?

- **Blue/Green Update with Canary Traffic Shifting**
    
    When you implement canary traffic shifting, you can test a portion of your endpoint traffic on the new fleet while the old fleet continues to serve the rest of the traffic. This testing step acts as a safety measure to validate the new fleet’s functionality before directing all traffic to it. This approach offers the benefits of a blue/green deployment, and the additional canary feature allows you to ensure that the new (green) fleet can handle traffic before it takes on 100% of the load.
    
    The portion of your green fleet that begins to receive traffic is called the canary, and you can choose the size of this canary. It’s important to note that the canary size should be no more than 50% of the new fleet’s capacity. Once the testing period is completed and no pre-specified Amazon CloudWatch alarms are triggered, the rest of the traffic shifts from the old (blue) fleet to the green fleet. Canary traffic shifting offers an extra layer of safety during your deployment, as any issues with the updated model will only affect the canary without impacting the entire system.
    
    ![canary traffic shifting](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/canary-traffic-shifting-27Jan2025.png)
    
    This strategy involves an initial phase where a fixed, small percentage of the traffic is routed to the new model (referred to as the “canary”). This allows the new model to be monitored with a limited amount of traffic. If the new model performs well during this phase, the remaining traffic is then switched to the new model.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Blue/Green Update with Canary Traffic Shifting.**
    
    The option that says: **Blue/Green Update with All At Once Traffic Shifting** is incorrect. This strategy primarily involves switching all traffic from the old model to the new model at once. While it can be faster, it doesn’t provide the opportunity to monitor the new model’s performance with a small portion of traffic first. This increases the risk of introducing issues if the new model has problems.
    
    The option that says: **Blue/Green Update with Linear Traffic Shifting** is incorrect. This strategy typically involves gradually shifting traffic to the new model linearly, but it doesn’t start with a small portion like the canary strategy. Instead, it increases traffic to the new model at a steady rate. While it reduces risk compared to an all-at-once shift, it doesn’t offer the same level of initial caution as the canary approach.
    
    The option that says: **Auto-Rollback Configuration and Monitoring** is incorrect because this is not a deployment strategy but rather a feature that can be used in conjunction with deployment strategies. Auto-rollback automatically reverts to the old model if the new model’s performance degrades. While useful, it doesn’t describe the gradual traffic-shifting process
    
- Blue/Green Update with Linear Traffic Shifting
- Auto-Rollback Configuration and Monitoring
- Blue/Green Update with All At Once Traffic Shifting

A Machine Learning team uses AWS for various machine learning workflows. This includes training and batch inference workflows with Amazon SageMaker Pipelines. The team also uses Amazon S3 buckets for data storage and AWS Glue tables for data cataloging. The team wants to manage and track costs through AWS Cost Center for each project.

Which of the following solutions will help the team achieve this goal?

- Rely on AWS-generated tags, such as the `aws:createdBy` tag, to automatically categorize and track costs for Amazon SageMaker Pipelines, Amazon S3 buckets, and AWS Glue tables in AWS Cost Explorer.
- Enable AWS Cost Allocation Reports to automatically generate tags for all machine learning resources and track costs in AWS Cost Explorer.
- Leverage AWS Budgets to set up cost and usage budgets for each project. Use the budget reports to track costs for Amazon SageMaker Pipelines, Amazon S3 buckets, and AWS Glue tables.
- **Manually create user-defined tags, including a project identifier tag, for resources like Amazon SageMaker Pipelines, Amazon S3 buckets, and AWS Glue tables, then activate these tags in the AWS Billing and Cost Management console to track costs in AWS Cost Explorer.**
    
    A tag is a label assigned to an AWS resource by either you or AWS. Each tag is composed of a key and a value, with each tag key being unique for a given resource and having only one associated value. Tags help you organize your resources, while cost allocation tags enable detailed tracking of your AWS expenses. Once you activate cost allocation tags, AWS uses them to categorize your resource costs in your cost allocation report, simplifying the process of tracking and managing your AWS expenditures.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/user-aws-defined-tags-091224.png)
    
    AWS offers two types of cost allocation tags: AWS-generated tags and user-defined tags. AWS or AWS Marketplace ISVs define, create, and apply AWS-generated tags on your behalf. In contrast, you are responsible for defining, creating, and applying user-defined tags. Both types of tags must be activated separately before they can be displayed in Cost Explorer or included in a cost allocation report.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/aws-tag-editor-091224.png)
    
    Tag Editor allows you to efficiently manage tags, which are key-value pairs used as metadata to organize your AWS resources. Most AWS resources support adding tags during their creation. Examples of such resources include an Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2) instance, an Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) bucket, or a secret in AWS Secrets Manager.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Manually create user-defined tags, including a project identifier tag, for resources like Amazon SageMaker Pipelines, Amazon S3 buckets, and AWS Glue tables, then activate these tags in the AWS Billing and Cost Management console to track costs in AWS Cost Explorer.** This option allows you to create specific tags that align with your organizational structure and reporting needs. By tagging resources with a project identifier, you can track costs accurately for each project. Activating these tags in the AWS Billing and Cost Management console (Cost Allocation tags) enables you to use AWS Cost Explorer to analyze and visualize the costs associated with each project. This method provides detailed and customizable cost tracking.
    
    The option that says: **Enable AWS Cost Allocation Reports to automatically generate tags for all machine learning resources and track costs in AWS Cost Explorer** is incorrect because AWS Cost Allocation Reports do not automatically generate tags for resources. You need to manually create and apply tags to your resources. Cost Allocation Reports can help you understand your costs, but they rely on the tags you have already set up.
    
    The option that says: **Leverage AWS Budgets to set up cost and usage budgets for each project, and use the budget reports to track costs for Amazon SageMaker Pipelines, Amazon S3 buckets, and AWS Glue tables** is incorrect. While AWS Budgets is typically used for setting cost and usage limits and receiving alerts, it does not provide the detailed cost tracking and categorization by project that user-defined tags and AWS Cost Explorer offer. Budgets are more about monitoring and controlling costs rather than detailed tracking and reporting.
    
    The option that says: **Rely on AWS-generated tags, such as the `aws:createdBy` tag, to automatically categorize and track costs for Amazon SageMaker Pipelines, Amazon S3 buckets, and AWS Glue tables in AWS Cost Explorer** is incorrect because AWS-generated tags like `aws:createdBy` can provide some level of cost tracking by indicating who created a resource. However, these tags are automatically applied and are primarily used to filter resources by their creator. They do not offer the detailed, project-based cost tracking needed for comprehensive cost management. User-defined tags, on the other hand, allow you to create specific tags that align with your project identifiers and organizational structure, providing greater flexibility and precision in tracking costs by project.
    

A machine learning (ML) engineer must build a model using Amazon SageMaker AI. The model will use sales transaction data to categorize customers into different behavior-based groups. The engineer needs to test various algorithms and ensure that the model runs and results are tracked for evaluation.

Which solution will meet these requirements with minimal manual configuration?

- Write a custom training script in a SageMaker-managed notebook instance and manually track the model's performance using external tools.
- **Implement SageMaker's built-in algorithms for training the model and leverage SageMaker Experiments to automatically track and analyze the results.**
    
    Amazon SageMaker AI provides a variety of built-in algorithms that make training machine learning models more efficient because they are pre-optimized for various tasks and require minimal configuration. These algorithms are directly integrated with SageMaker’s platform, enabling the machine learning engineer to focus on experimenting with different models without worrying about the overhead of custom code or infrastructure management. With **SageMaker Experiments** allows the engineer to automatically track each model’s run, hyperparameters, and results, making it easier to evaluate multiple algorithms and choose the best-performing model. SageMaker Experiments streamlines the experimenting process, allowing for easier organizing and comparison of various runs.
    
    ![amazon-sagemaker-built-in-algorithms](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-amazon-sagemaker-built-in-algorithms-12Sept2024.png)
    
    Leveraging **SageMaker built-in algorithms**reduces the effort needed to configure environments, manage dependencies, or track model results manually. Since SageMaker built-in algorithms and SageMaker Experiments are designed to work together seamlessly, this approach enables minimal manual intervention while providing a robust solution for algorithm comparison and performance tracking.
    
    ![amazon-sagemaker-XGBoost](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/amazon-sagemaker-XGBoost-12Sept2024.png)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Implement SageMaker’s built-in algorithms for training the model and leverage SageMaker Experiments to automatically track and analyze the results.**
    
    The option that says: **Deploy a pre-trained model from SageMaker JumpStart and use SageMaker Model Monitor to evaluate the model’s performance** is incorrect. SageMaker JumpStart is designed for quick deployment of pre-built models, but it doesn’t allow testing different algorithms and custom training, which is required in the scenario. Model Monitor is for monitoring deployed models in production, not for training evaluation.
    
    The option that says: **Write a custom training script in a SageMaker-managed notebook instance and manually track the model’s performance using external tools** is incorrect. While this approach works, it involves manual tracking and more configuration effort, which contradicts the requirement for minimal manual configuration.
    
    The option that says: **Build a custom container with the necessary algorithms and push it to Amazon ECR. Use SageMaker Experiments to track the training runs and outcomes** is incorrect because building a custom container mostly requires significant manual effort to configure the environment and manage dependencies. While SageMaker Experiments can still track the runs, using built-in algorithms eliminates the need for complex container setups, thus reducing effort.
    
- Deploy a pre-trained model from SageMaker JumpStart and use SageMaker Model Monitor to evaluate the model's performance.
- Build a custom container with the necessary algorithms and push it to Amazon ECR. Use SageMaker Experiments to track the training runs and outcomes.

A data science team uses Amazon SageMaker AI to predict loan approvals with an XGBoost classification model on an imbalanced tabular dataset. The target column, ‘approved,’ is labeled 1 for approval and 0 for rejection. During the hyperparameter tuning phase, the team must choose the best evaluation metric to assess model performance.

Which evaluation metric is the most suitable for this use case?

- `validation: mae`
- **`validation:f1`**
    
    **XGBoost (eXtreme Gradient Boosting)** is a widely used and efficient open-source implementation of the gradient-boosted trees technique. Gradient boosting is a supervised learning approach that attempts to accurately predict a target variable by merging estimates from smaller models.
    
    Automatic model tuning, also known as hyperparameter tuning, determines the ideal level version of a model by performing a series of jobs that test a variety of hyperparameters on your training and validation datasets.
    
    In this use case, the team is building a binary classification model to predict loan approval. Selecting the right evaluation metric is crucial in determining how well the model is performing, especially in situations where class imbalance is common (i.e., many more loan rejections than approvals, or vice versa). The key consideration is the balance between precision and recall, as it’s important to reduce both false positives and false negatives.
    
    ![amazon-sagemaker-XGBoost](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/amazon-sagemaker-XGBoost-12Sept2024.png)
    
    The **F1 score** combines precision and recall into a single metric by computing their harmonic mean, making it a good choice when dealing with imbalanced datasets. It gives a better measure of the incorrectly classified cases than accuracy.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **validation:f1.**
    
    The option that says: **`validation:accuracy`**is incorrect because accuracy alone might not be the best metric in cases of imbalanced data. High accuracy could be misleading if the model predicts the majority class well but performs poorly on the minority class.
    
    The option that says: **`validation: mae`**  is incorrect because MAE (Mean Absolute Error) is primarily used for regression tasks, not for classification problems as the case in the scenario. MAE simply measures the average magnitude of errors in continuous predictions, which does not apply to binary classification.
    
    The option that says: **`validation:map`** is incorrect because MAP (Mean Average Precision) is more suitable for ranking tasks or information retrieval scenarios, not binary classification.
    
- `validation:map`
- `validation:accuracy`

A healthcare organization’s compliance policy requires that its Amazon SageMaker notebook instances do not have direct internet access. To meet this requirement, the organization deployed the notebook instance in a private VPC subnet. However, the data science team occasionally needs temporary internet access to download updates.

Which solution will allow the SageMaker notebook instance to receive necessary updates while adhering to the compliance policy?

- **Set up a NAT gateway in the VPC and update route tables to route internet traffic through the NAT gateway. Modify security groups and network ACLs to permit outbound access.**
    
    A **NAT Gateway** is a managed service that allows resources in a private subnet to connect to the Internet or other AWS services but prevents the Internet from initiating connections with those resources.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-nat-gateway-13-09-2024.png)
    
    Amazon SageMaker notebook instances in private subnets can access the Internet through a NAT gateway without violating compliance rules prohibiting direct internet access. The NAT gateway enables outbound internet access from the private subnet while blocking inbound connections from the Internet, maintaining the security and compliance policies of the healthcare organization.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Set up a NAT gateway in the VPC and update route tables to route internet traffic through the NAT gateway. Modify security groups and network ACLs to permit outbound access.**
    
    The option says: **Deploy an AWS Direct Connect connection to a private network, and use it to access external resources** is incorrect. AWS Direct Connect is a cloud service solution that establishes a dedicated network connection between an on-premises network and an AWS Direct Connect location. It is primarily used for establishing private connectivity between an on-premises data center and AWS, not for accessing external resources like software updates.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS PrivateLink to establish a private connection between the SageMaker notebook instance and the proxy server** is incorrect. AWS PrivateLink is a service that allows you to privately access services hosted on the AWS network without exposing your VPC to the public Internet. It is primarily used for securely accessing AWS services or services hosted by other AWS customers, not for accessing external resources like software updates.
    
    The option that says: **Enable the SageMaker parameter `EnableNetworkIsolation` for the notebook instances, and configure a proxy server within the VPC to route internet traffic** is incorrect. While enabling the `EnableNetworkIsolation` parameter for SageMaker notebook instances can typically help isolate the instances from the Internet, it does not provide a solution for accessing external resources like software updates. Additionally, configuring a proxy server within the VPC is not recommended for this use case, as it would still require the proxy server to have direct internet access, which violates the compliance policy.
    
- Enable the SageMaker parameter `EnableNetworkIsolation` for the notebook instances, and configure a proxy server within the VPC to route internet traffic.
- Use AWS PrivateLink to establish a private connection between the SageMaker notebook instance and the proxy server.
- Deploy an AWS Direct Connect connection to a private network, and use it to access external resources.

A machine learning engineer is building a predictive model to estimate car values using the XGBoost algorithm in Amazon SageMaker AI. The dataset used for training is in tabular format, with a column named “car_value” that indicates the market price for each vehicle. As part of the model optimization process, the engineer sets up hyperparameter tuning and must select the most suitable evaluation metric.

How should the performance of this model be evaluated?

- Use `validation: accuracy`
- Choose `validation:ndcg`
- **Use `validation: rmse`**
    
    Amazon SageMaker AI supports multiple evaluation metrics depending on the type of machine learning task, such as classification or regression.
