An e-commerce company stores transactional and customer behavior data in JSON and Parquet formats on Amazon S3. They need a solution that allows SQL-based querying of the data and provides interactive dashboards for visualizing the results. The solution should also incorporate machine learning to forecast trends.

Which solution will meet this requirement with the least amount of development effort?

- **Use Amazon Athena to query the data in Amazon S3, integrate with Amazon QuickSight for interactive dashboards, and leverage Amazon QuickSight ML Insights for forecasting trends.**
    
    **Amazon Athena** is an interactive query service that enables users to run SQL-based queries directly on data stored in Amazon S3 without complex ETL processes. Athena supports many file formats, including JSON and Parquet, making it ideal for querying structured and semi-structured data. Since Athena is serverless, users don’t need to manage infrastructure, and they only pay for the queries they run. Athena’s seamless integration with Amazon QuickSight allows users to build interactive and visually rich dashboards, providing insights from data for various use cases, such as customer segmentation and sales trend analysis.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-quicksight-09-19-2024.png)
    
    **Amazon QuickSight** is a scalable business intelligence service that can connect to various data sources, including Athena, to create visually appealing and interactive dashboards. It enables non-technical users, like marketing teams, to explore data and generate insights from customer behavior and transaction data.
    
    **Amazon QuickSight ML Insights** uses machine learning to uncover hidden insights and trends, helping organizations identify key drivers and forecast business metrics. ML Insightsffers three main features: ML-powered anomaly detection, ML-powered forecasting, and autonarratives. The anomaly detection feature leverages Amazon’s Random Cut Forest algorithm to detect outliers and identify the top contributors to any significant changes, such as spikes in sales or dips in website traffic. With ML-powered forecasting, non-technical users can confidently forecast metrics by handling complex scenarios like seasonality and outliers. Autonarratives enhance dashboards with plain language descriptions, enabling users to quickly interpret data and make faster, informed decisions.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use Amazon Athena to query the data in Amazon S3, integrate with Amazon QuickSight for interactive dashboards, and leverage Amazon QuickSight ML Insights for forecasting trends.**
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Redshift Spectrum to query data in S3, create visualizations with Amazon QuickSight, and utilize Redshift ML to integrate an Autoregressive Integrated Moving Average (ARIMA) model to forecast trends** is incorrect. While Amazon Redshift Spectrum can query data in S3, setting up Redshift for this scenario introduces unnecessary complexity. Additionally, with Redshift ML, you’d still need to train and deploy a machine learning model, which requires specialized knowledge and more development time.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon S3 Object Lambda to process and transform the data during retrieval, integrate the results with Amazon SageMaker for machine learning, and use Amazon CloudWatch for dashboards** is incorrect because S3 Object Lambda is designed to modify or process data during retrieval but is not optimized for large-scale SQL queries or analytics across large datasets, which is essential for customer segmentation and predictive analytics. Additionally, Amazon CloudWatch is primarily used for monitoring and is not suitable for creating interactive business intelligence dashboards, which are required for visualizing customer clusters and sales trends.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Glue DataBrew for SQL-based queries and connect the data to Amazon ElasticSearch for real-time dashboard integration** is incorrect. AWS Glue DataBrew simply focuses on preparing and cleaning data, not performing SQL queries. It does not have the necessary capabilities for querying large datasets stored in S3 using SQL. ElasticSearch, while effective for search-based analytics and indexing, is not optimized for machine learning integration or interactive dashboards. It is primarily used for full-text search and log analysis, which is not aligned with the need for customer segmentation, predictive analytics, and real-time insights.
    
- Use AWS Glue DataBrew for SQL-based queries and connect the data to Amazon ElasticSearch for real-time dashboard integration.
- Use Amazon Redshift Spectrum to query data in S3, create visualizations with Amazon QuickSight, and utilize Redshift ML to integrate an Autoregressive Integrated Moving Average (ARIMA) model to forecast trends.
- Use Amazon S3 Object Lambda to process and transform the data during retrieval, integrate the results with Amazon SageMaker for machine learning, and use Amazon CloudWatch for dashboards.

A financial institution is developing a machine learning model to detect fraudulent transactions. The training data is highly imbalanced, with a significantly larger number of non-fraudulent cases compared to fraudulent ones. The model has a high rate of false negatives, missing many fraudulent transactions.

Which of the following options will help to improve the model’s performance in detecting fraudulent transactions?

- Remove non-fraudulent cases to balance the dataset.
- **Oversample the fraudulent cases using Synthetic Minority Oversampling Technique (SMOTE).**
    
    Balancing datasets with underrepresented categories can enhance the performance of binary classification models. You can achieve this balance using the **Balance data** operation with one of the following methods:
    
    **– Random oversampling:** This method randomly duplicates samples from the minority category. For instance, if fraudulent transactions make up only 10% of your data, this technique will duplicate fraud cases multiple times to achieve an equal proportion of fraudulent and non-fraudulent cases.
    
    **– Random undersampling:** Similar to random oversampling, this method randomly removes samples from the overrepresented category to achieve the desired proportion of samples.
    
    **– Synthetic Minority Oversampling Technique (SMOTE):** This technique generates new synthetic samples by interpolating between existing samples from the underrepresented category. For more details on SMOTE, refer to the provided description.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/sagemaker-data-wrangler-091924.png)
    
    Oversampling the fraudulent cases using SMOTE helps to balance the dataset by generating synthetic samples of the minority class (fraudulent transactions). This can improve the model’s ability to detect fraudulent transactions and reduce the rate of false negatives. By having more examples of fraudulent transactions, the model can learn better patterns and distinctions between fraudulent and non-fraudulent cases.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Oversample the fraudulent cases using Synthetic Minority Oversampling Technique (SMOTE).**
    
    The option that says: **Oversample the non-fraudulent cases using Synthetic Minority Oversampling Technique (SMOTE)** is incorrect because oversampling the non-fraudulent cases would further increase the imbalance in the dataset, making the problem just worse. The goal is to balance the dataset by increasing the number of fraudulent cases, not the non-fraudulent ones.
    
    The option that says: **Remove non-fraudulent cases to balance the dataset** is incorrect. This method would result in a loss of valuable data. This could lead to a model that is not well-trained on the variety of non-fraudulent transactions, potentially reducing its overall performance and generalizability.
    
    The option that says: **Randomly undersample the non-fraudulent cases** is incorrect because this can typically lead to a loss of important information and may increase the variance of the classifier. This approach might discard useful samples, which could negatively impact the model’s performance.
    
- Oversample the non-fraudulent cases using Synthetic Minority Oversampling Technique (SMOTE).
- Randomly undersample the non-fraudulent cases.

A data scientist at a healthcare company has developed multiple machine-learning models using R. The data scientist wants to deploy these models on Amazon SageMaker. The main goal is to minimize operational overhead while ensuring efficient deployment and management of the R models.

Which solution should the data scientist choose?

- Use SageMaker’s built-in R kernel to develop and deploy the models directly from a Jupyter notebook.
- **Create a custom R Dockerfile, build the Docker image locally, push it to Amazon ECR, and use it to create a SageMaker endpoint.**
    
    SageMaker notebook instances come with a pre-installed R kernel, which includes the reticulate library. This library provides an R to Python interface, enabling you to utilize the features of the SageMaker Python SDK directly within an R script. Additionally, Amazon SageMaker offers RStudio as a fully managed integrated development environment (IDE) within the SageMaker domain. With this integration, you can launch an RStudio environment to run your RStudio workflows on SageMaker resources seamlessly.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/amazon-sagemaker-endpoint-092124.png)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create a custom R Dockerfile, build the Docker image locally, push it to Amazon ECR, and use it to create a SageMaker endpoint.** This option allows the data scientist to package all necessary R dependencies and the models into a Docker image. By pushing this image to Amazon ECR (Elastic Container Registry), it can be easily managed and deployed on SageMaker. This approach minimizes operational overhead because it leverages Docker’s containerization capabilities, ensuring a consistent and reproducible environment for the R models.
    
    The option that says: **Manually install R and all necessary dependencies on an EC2 instance, then use SageMaker’s local mode to deploy the models** is incorrect because this option involves a lot of manual setup and maintenance, which typically increases operational overhead. Managing dependencies and configurations manually on an EC2 instance can be error-prone and time-consuming, making it less efficient than Docker containers.
    
    The option that says: **Use the SageMaker Python SDK to convert the R models into Python models and deploy them using SageMaker’s built-in Python containers** is incorrect because converting R models to Python models can be complex and may not always be feasible, especially if the models rely on R-specific libraries and functionalities. This approach also introduces additional steps and potential compatibility issues, increasing operational overhead.
    
    The option that says: **Use SageMaker’s built-in R kernel to develop and deploy the models directly from a Jupyter notebook** is incorrect. While using SageMaker’s built-in R kernel might seem convenient, it is not primarily designed for the production deployment of models. This approach is more suitable for development and experimentation rather than efficient and scalable deployment, leading to higher operational overhead in a production environment.
    
- Use the SageMaker Python SDK to convert the R models into Python models and deploy them using SageMaker’s built-in Python containers.
- Manually install R and all necessary dependencies on an EC2 instance, then use SageMaker’s local mode to deploy the models.

An ML Engineer is tasked with deploying a machine learning model to serve predictions. The model processes image files ranging from 8 MB to 15 MB. Each image requires complex computations that can take up to 25 minutes to process. The endpoint must be accessible within a private VPC for security compliance. Additionally, the solution must be cost-effective and not incur idle charges.

Which deployment option will meet these requirements?

- Deploy the model using a SageMaker Real-time inference endpoint with VPC configuration.
- Deploy the model using a SageMaker endpoint with built-in model optimization.
- **Deploy the model using a SageMaker Asynchronous inference endpoint with VPC configuration.**
    
    **Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference** is a deployment option for machine learning models that handle infrequent, large, or long-running inference requests. It allows models to scale efficiently without needing real-time responses, making it ideal for scenarios where cold starts and queuing are acceptable. Asynchronous Inference accepts inference requests into an internal queue, processing them as compute resources become available. Once the Inference is complete, the result is stored in an Amazon S3 bucket, and the user can be notified via Amazon SNS or an HTTP callback. This method optimizes resource usage by scaling instances down when idle, significantly reducing deployment costs for workloads with sporadic inference needs.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-asynchronous-vpc-config-sample-19-09-24.png)
    
    SageMaker Asynchronous inference is designed for workloads that require long-running computations or large payloads, making it suitable for processing large image files (8 MB to 15 MB) with complex computations that can take up to 25 minutes. Additionally, SageMaker supports deploying endpoints within a private VPC for security compliance. Asynchronous inference also allows for cost-effective scaling by automatically scaling down to zero instances when there are no pending requests, avoiding idle charges.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Deploy the model using a SageMaker Asynchronous inference endpoint with VPC configuration.**
    
    The option that says: **Deploy the model using a SageMaker Real-time inference endpoint with VPC configuration** is incorrect. Real-time inference endpoints are optimized for low-latency predictions and are not ideal for tasks requiring long processing times, as they would incur costs while waiting, even when idle.
    
    The option that says: **Deploy the model using a SageMaker Multi-model endpoint with VPC support** is incorrect because SageMaker Multi-model endpoints are primarily designed to host multiple models behind a single endpoint. While Multi-model endpoints support VPC configuration, they are not specifically designed for long-running computations or large payloads, and they may not provide the cost-effectiveness required for the given scenario.
    
    The option that says: **Deploy the model using a SageMaker endpoint with built-in model optimization** is incorrect because SageMaker’s built-in model optimization features are focused on optimizing the model for performance and cost-effectiveness during inference, but they do not address the specific requirements of long-running computations, large payloads, or cost-effectiveness for idle instances. Model optimization techniques like quantization, compilation, and automatic model parallelism are primarily aimed at improving inference latency and throughput.
    
- Deploy the model using a SageMaker Multi-model endpoint with VPC support.

A company uses an Amazon SageMaker endpoint to deploy an ML model that determines charges based on factors like age and driving history. Currently, the model assigns more importance to the applicant’s age compared to the driving history. The company also utilizes AWS CloudWatch to monitor other aspects of its infrastructure.

An ML engineer wants to implement a feature that alerts if the production model prioritizes driving history over age when calculating insurance costs.

Which approach will fulfill these requirements?

- **Use the `ModelExplainabilityMonitor` class to create a SHAP baseline. Deploy this baseline to SageMaker Model Monitor, then periodically monitor for feature attribution drift and capture any violations. Set up Amazon CloudWatch alerts to receive notifications.**
    
    **Amazon SageMaker AI** has a powerful monitoring system called SageMaker Model Monitor, which allows for continuous checks of installed ML models. For this specific scenario, the ideal strategy is to monitor feature attribution drift, which refers to changes in how input characteristics contribute to model predictions over time. The `ModelExplainabilityMonitor` leverages SHAP (Shapley Additive Explanations) values to track the contribution of each feature. This feature attribution monitoring ensures that if a model begins to emphasize one input (such as driving history) more than another (like age), it can be detected and trigger alerts in Amazon CloudWatch. This is particularly useful for models deployed in production that need to maintain consistent decision-making criteria over time.
    
    ![SageMaker Model Monitor SHAP Baseline](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/Amazon-Sagemaker-Model-Monitor-12June2025.jpg)
    
    SHAP baselines allow ML engineers to create an explainability baseline for how each feature should contribute to the model’s predictions. SageMaker Model Monitor then compares this baseline with ongoing model outputs to detect feature attribution drift. If a significant drift is detected, such as driving history becoming more important than age, then `ModelExplainabilityMonitor` can raise a violation and trigger an alert in CloudWatch. This allows proactive detection of changes in model behavior and ensures that decisions remain aligned with business requirements.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use the `ModelExplainabilityMonitor` class to create a SHAP baseline. Deploy this baseline to SageMaker Model Monitor, then periodically monitor for feature attribution drift and capture any violations. Set up Amazon CloudWatch alerts to receive notifications.**
    
    The option that says: **Create a baseline for monitoring bias using the `ModelBiasMonitor` class. Deploy this baseline to SageMaker Model Monitor and periodically check for bias drift. Set up Amazon CloudWatch to send alerts when violations are detected** is incorrect because this class simply focuses on identifying bias in predictions related to specific attributes such as gender or race, not changes in feature attribution between inputs like age and driving history. This option does not address the specific requirement of detecting feature attribution drift.
    
    The option that says: **Use the `ModelDataQualityMonitor` class to establish a baseline for input data quality. Deploy the baseline to SageMaker Model Monitor to track changes in data patterns and set up notifications in Amazon CloudWatch for any detected deviations** is incorrect because this class is primarily used to track the quality of input data and its distribution but does not monitor the importance of features in model predictions. This solution would not detect shifts in feature importance.
    
    The option that says: **Create a performance baseline using the `ModelPerformanceMonitor` class. Deploy this baseline to SageMaker Model Monitor and configure it to track the model’s prediction accuracy and trigger alerts if any major variations occur** is incorrect because this class only tracks the overall performance of the model (such as accuracy or precision) but does not provide insight into how individual features contribute to the predictions, making it irrelevant to feature attribution drift.
    
- Use the `ModelDataQualityMonitor`class to establish a baseline for input data quality. Deploy the baseline to SageMaker Model Monitor to track changes in data patterns and set up notifications in Amazon CloudWatch for any detected deviations.
- Create a performance baseline using the `ModelPerformanceMonitor` class. Deploy this baseline to SageMaker Model Monitor and configure it to track the model’s prediction accuracy and trigger alerts if any major variations occur.
- Create a baseline for monitoring bias using the `ModelBiasMonitor` class. Deploy this baseline to SageMaker Model Monitor and periodically check for bias drift. Set up Amazon CloudWatch to send alerts when violations are detected.

A Machine Learning Engineer team must fine-tune a BERT-based Natural Language Processing (NLP) model using Amazon SageMaker. The team has a large dataset (150 GB) of text files stored in Amazon S3. The fine-tuning process is taking longer than expected, and the team needs to optimize the data access method to speed up the model training time without increasing the operational complexity.

What solution requires the LEAST operational overhead to meet the objectives?

- Enable pipe mode in the training configuration to stream data directly from S3 to the training instance.
- **Configure fast file mode in the SageMaker training job and ensure the training instance has enough storage for the entire dataset.**
    
    **Amazon SageMaker** provides different data access modes for training jobs, which determine how the training data is loaded and accessed by the training instances. The available modes include:
    
    **File Mode**:
    
    – In File Mode, the entire training dataset is downloaded from Amazon S3 to the local storage of the training instance before the training job starts.
    
    – This mode is suitable for smaller datasets that can fit entirely on the instance’s local storage.
    
    – The training algorithm reads the data from the local storage during the training process.
    
    **Pipe Mode**:
    
    – In Pipe Mode, the training data is streamed directly from Amazon S3 to the training instance during the training job.
    
    – The data is not stored on the local storage of the instance; instead, it is streamed to the training algorithm as it is being read.
    
    – This mode is efficient for large datasets that cannot fit on the instance’s local storage or when the dataset needs to be processed in a streaming fashion.
    
    **Fast File Mode**:
    
    – Fast File Mode optimizes data access for large datasets stored in Amazon S3.
    
    – It combines the benefits of both File Mode and Pipe Mode.
    
    – Before training begins, Fast File Mode identifies the data source files in Amazon S3 but does not download them immediately.
    
    – It allows the model to begin training before the entire dataset has finished loading, thereby decreasing the startup time.
    
    – As the training progresses, the entire dataset is loaded in parallel from Amazon S3 to the local storage of the training instance.
    
    – Fast File Mode provides the flexibility to access entire files like File Mode, while also offering better performance of Pipe Mode by streaming data directly to the training algorithm.
    
    – To leverage Fast File Mode effectively, the training instance must have enough storage capacity to accommodate the entire dataset.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-s3-fast-file-mode-20-09-24.png)
    
    Given the requirements to speed up training without increasing operational complexity, **Fast File Mode** is the most efficient option. It offers the performance improvements of data streaming and minimal operational overhead because no code changes are required. Additionally, the Fast File Mode “gives SageMaker the flexibility to access entire files in the same way as file mode,” which can be beneficial for certain workloads, such as Natural Language Processing (NLP) models like BERT, where accessing entire files or documents may be necessary.
    
    By combining the improved performance, reduced startup time, and minimal operational overhead, Fast File Mode emerges as the most efficient solution for optimizing the data access method for fine-tuning the BERT-based NLP model using the large dataset stored in Amazon S3.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Configure fast file mode in the SageMaker training job and ensure the training instance has enough storage for the entire dataset.**
    
    The option that says: **Enable pipe mode in the training configuration to stream data directly from S3 to the training instance** is incorrect. Pipe mode is designed for streaming data directly from S3, which works well for scenarios where data can be processed sequentially. However, with large text datasets required for fine-tuning an NLP model, Fast File Mode is more suitable because it allows Amazon SageMaker to load portions of the files on-demand with higher performance, providing a faster and more efficient solution. Fast File Mode also reduces startup time and operational complexity by balancing the advantages of both streaming and direct file access, which are not available in Pipe Mode alone.
    
    The option that says: **Configure file mode in the SageMaker training job and ensure the training instance has enough storage for the entire dataset** is incorrect because File Mode typically requires downloading the entire dataset to the local storage of the training instance before the training job starts. For large datasets like 150 GB, this can significantly increase the training time due to the time required for downloading the data.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Glue to preprocess the dataset and store it in the Amazon Elastic File System (Amazon EFS)** is incorrect. While using AWS Glue and Amazon EFS can be useful for complex preprocessing or shared storage, this only introduces unnecessary operational complexity for this use case and does not optimize the data access during training.
    
- Configure file mode in the SageMaker training job and ensure the training instance has enough storage for the entire dataset.
- Use AWS Glue to preprocess the dataset and store it in the Amazon Elastic File System (Amazon EFS).

An ML engineer is deploying a real-time object detection model on vehicle dashcams. The dashcams have limited computing power and need the model to run with low latency for quick responses. Neo optimizes the model to run faster without compromising accuracy, ensuring that objects can be detected and processed quickly for timely decision-making on the road. This combination of speed, efficiency, and accuracy makes it the ideal choice for deploying real-time object detection models in vehicle systems.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Amazon SageMaker Neo.**
    
    The option that says: **Amazon SageMaker Pipelines** is incorrect because it is primarily used for automating and orchestrating end-to-end machine learning workflows, not for model optimization or low-latency deployment.
    
    The option that says: **Amazon Lex** is incorrect because it is only used for building conversational interfaces, such as chatbots, and is not suited for deploying fraud detection models.

An ML Engineer is developing a sentiment analysis model using TensorFlow and PyTorch. The model must classify real-time customer review data collected from social media platforms. The data will be ingested into Amazon S3 and will undergo preprocessing steps such as tokenization, stop-word removal, and vectorization before being used for model training. The engineer requires a scalable and serverless solution to manage the preprocessing and model training tasks.

Which combination of steps will meet these requirements with the LEAST operational overhead? (Select THREE.)

- **Use Amazon SageMaker Processing jobs to handle the data preprocessing steps like tokenization and vectorization.**
- **Use AWS Step Functions to orchestrate the preprocessing and model training workflows in a serverless manner.**
- Use Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow (Amazon MWAA) to manage and orchestrate the preprocessing and model training workflows.
- Use AWS Lambda to preprocess the text data and store the results back into Amazon S3.
- **Enable Amazon S3 Event Notifications to trigger the preprocessing workflows upon new data uploads.**
- Deploy an AWS Lambda function to monitor and detect newly uploaded files in Amazon S3.
- **Evaluation**
    
    **Amazon SageMaker Processing jobs** are designed to handle data preprocessing, post-processing, and model evaluation tasks. They provide a scalable and serverless environment for running custom data processing scripts or built-in algorithms. By using SageMaker Processing jobs, the ML Engineer can leverage the scalability and managed infrastructure of Amazon SageMaker without worrying about provisioning and managing compute resources.
    
    **Amazon S3 Event Notifications** allow you to receive notifications when certain events occur in your S3 bucket, such as object creation or deletion. By enabling S3 Event Notifications, the preprocessing workflow can be automatically triggered whenever new customer review data is uploaded to the S3 bucket. This ensures that the preprocessing steps are executed in a timely manner, enabling real-time processing of the incoming data.
    
    **AWS Step Functions** is a serverless function orchestrator that allows you to coordinate multiple AWS services into business workflows. In this case, Step Functions can be used to orchestrate the entire preprocessing and model training workflow, including triggering the SageMaker Processing jobs for preprocessing, managing the data flow between different steps, and potentially invoking other AWS services like SageMaker Training for model training. Step Functions provides a visual representation of the workflow, making it easier to manage and monitor the entire process.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-stepfunction-sagemaker-sample-20-09-2024.png)
    
    By leveraging the combination of Amazon SageMaker Processing jobs, Amazon S3 Event Notifications, and AWS Step Functions, the ML Engineer can build a scalable and serverless solution for preprocessing and training the sentiment analysis model with minimal operational overhead.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Use Amazon SageMaker Processing jobs to handle the data preprocessing steps like tokenization and vectorization.**
    
    **– Enable Amazon S3 Event Notifications to trigger the preprocessing workflows upon new data uploads.**
    
    **– Use AWS Step Functions to orchestrate the preprocessing and model training workflows in a serverless manner.**
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Lambda to preprocess the text data and store the results back into Amazon S3** is incorrect. While AWS Lambda can be used for data preprocessing tasks, it is not the most suitable choice for this scenario. AWS Lambda is primarily designed for short-lived, event-driven tasks and has limitations on execution time and memory. For large-scale data preprocessing tasks, Amazon SageMaker Processing jobs or AWS Glue are more appropriate choices.
    
    The option that says: **Deploy an AWS Lambda function to monitor and detect newly uploaded files in Amazon S3** is incorrect as it only introduces unnecessary overhead. S3 Event Notifications can trigger workflows without needing a Lambda function to monitor file uploads manually.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow (Amazon MWAA) to manage and orchestrate the preprocessing and model training workflows** is incorrect. Amazon MWAA is a managed workflow orchestration service but requires more configuration and is not serverless. Step Functions is a better fit for a lightweight, serverless orchestration option in this scenario.
    

A data scientist sets up an Amazon SageMaker inference endpoint with a target tracking scaling policy to optimize performance based on CPU utilization. However, during specific hours of the day, the traffic received by the endpoint spikes significantly, causing delays in response times. The goal is to implement a solution to handle these predictable traffic spikes more efficiently without over-provisioning resources during off-peak hours.

Which solution should the data scientist implement to address the traffic spikes efficiently?

- Add more instances to the target tracking scaling policy to handle the increased load.
- Increase the instance type of the SageMaker endpoint to a larger instance.
- Use a step scaling policy to adjust the number of instances based on predefined thresholds.
- **Implement a scheduled scaling policy to automatically scale the number of instances based on the time of day.**
    
    Amazon SageMaker offers automatic scaling (auto scaling) for your hosted models. This feature dynamically adjusts the number of instances allocated to a model based on workload changes. When the workload increases, auto scaling adds more instances. Conversely, when the workload decreases, auto scaling reduces the number of instances, ensuring you only pay for the resources you actually use.
    
    You can configure scheduled actions to perform scaling activities at specific times. These actions can be set to scale either once or on a recurring schedule. Once a scheduled action is executed, your scaling policy can continue to dynamically adjust scaling based on workload changes. Scheduled scaling can only be managed through the AWS CLI or the Application Auto Scaling API.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/sagemaker-scheduled-scaling-092124.png)
    
    A scheduled scaling policy enables automatic adjustment of the number of instances at specific times of the day. This is particularly useful for managing predictable traffic spikes, ensuring that additional resources are available during peak hours and scaled down during off-peak hours. This method helps in efficiently managing resources without over-provisioning, thereby optimizing costs and performance.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Implement a scheduled scaling policy to automatically scale the number of instances based on the time of day.**
    
    The option that says: **Increase the instance type of the SageMaker endpoint to a larger instance** is incorrect. While increasing the instance type to a larger instance might temporarily handle the increased load, it is not an efficient solution for predictable traffic spikes. This approach can lead to over-provisioning during off-peak hours, resulting in unnecessary costs.
    
    The option that says: **Add more instances to the target tracking scaling policy to handle the increased load** is incorrect because adding more instances to the target tracking scaling policy can typically help manage the increased load, but it does not address the issue of predictable traffic spikes efficiently. The target tracking policy adjusts resources based on real-time metrics, which might not be as effective in handling sudden, predictable spikes compared to a scheduled scaling policy.
    
    The option that says: **Use a step scaling policy to adjust the number of instances based on predefined thresholds** is incorrect because the step scaling policy primarily adjusts the number of instances based on predefined thresholds of metrics like CPU utilization. While this can help manage load, it is reactive rather than proactive. It may not be as efficient in handling predictable traffic spikes as a scheduled scaling policy, which preemptively adjusts resources based on known traffic patterns.
    

An ML Engineer works with a pre-trained machine learning model in Amazon SageMaker. The model was previously trained using SageMaker’s Automatic Model Tuning (AMT) with hyperparameter optimization. The engineer wants to retrain the model using new data to improve accuracy and reduce bias. They aim to leverage the results of the previous tuning job to save training time and cost while ensuring efficient optimization.

Which type of tuning job should the ML Engineer choose to meet these requirements?

- **Run a warm start tuning job by setting the type to `TRANSFER_LEARNING` and configure the early stopping parameter to `Auto`.**
    
    **Amazon SageMaker Automatic Model Tuning (AMT)** is a SageMaker feature that automates hyperparameter tuning. It uses machine learning algorithms to find the best hyperparameter values for a given model and dataset. AMT allows users to specify the maximum number of training jobs it should run during the tuning process. A higher number of training jobs may lead to better hyperparameter optimization but will also increase the training time and cost.
    
    SageMaker allows you to start a new hyperparameter tuning job using the results of a previous tuning job. This is known as a **warm starttuning job**. It can significantly reduce the time and cost required for hyperparameter tuning by starting from the best hyperparameter values found in the previous job.
    
    Warm start tuning has two configurations:
    
    **`IDENTICAL_DATA_AND_ALGORITHM`**: This warm start type is used when the new tuning job uses the same input data and algorithm as the previous job. It allows SageMaker to start the new tuning job from the best hyperparameter values in the previous job.
    
    **`TRANSFER_LEARNING`**: This warm start type is used when the new tuning job uses a dataset or algorithm different than the previous job. It allows SageMaker to transfer the knowledge gained from the previous job to the new job, potentially leading to faster convergence and better results.
    
    **`Auto`** for early stopping: This setting allows SageMaker to automatically determine the best time to stop the training process based on the model’s performance
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-hyperparameter-transfer-learning-23-09-24.png)
    
    Using `TRANSFER_LEARNING` in a warm start job allows the model to incorporate new data and new hyperparameter ranges without starting from scratch, making it the most suitable choice when new data is introduced, as in this case. Early stopping can further optimize the process by stopping training when no significant improvements are detected.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Run a warm start tuning job by setting the type to `TRANSFER_LEARNING` and configure the early stopping parameter to `Auto`.**
    
    The option that says: **Run a warm start tuning job by setting the type `IDENTICAL_DATA_AND_ALGORITHM` and configure the early stopping parameter to `Auto`** is incorrect because the `IDENTICAL_DATA_AND_ALGORITHM` setting is typically for cases where the dataset and algorithm remain unchanged. In the scenario, the training involves new data.
    
    The option that says: **Run a warm start tuning job by setting the type to `IDENTICAL_DATA_AND_ALGORITHM` and configure it to use the maximum number of training jobs** is incorrect. Similar to the previous option setting the warm start type to `IDENTICAL_DATA_AND_ALGORITHM` is simply not applicable when using new data. Additionally, configuring the tuning job to use the maximum number of training jobs can lead to unnecessary training time and cost
    
    The option that says: **Run a warm start tuning job by setting the type to `TRANSFER_LEARNING`and configure it to use the maximum number of training jobs** is incorrect. Setting the warm start type to `TRANSFER_LEARNING` is appropriate when using new data. However, configuring the tuning job to use the maximum number of training jobs may only lead to unnecessary computational resources and costs without providing any additional benefit in this scenario.
    
- Run a warm start tuning job by setting the type to `IDENTICAL_DATA_AND_ALGORITHM` and configure it to use the maximum number of training jobs.
- Run a warm start tuning job by setting the type `IDENTICAL_DATA_AND_ALGORITHM` and configure the early stopping parameter to `Auto`.
- Run a warm start tuning job by setting the type to `TRANSFER_LEARNING` and configure it to use the maximum number of training jobs.

A healthcare company is building a Cox model to predict patient survival times. The company aims to run an Amazon SageMaker training job using a pre-built scikit-learn container. The training dataset is stored in Amazon S3, and the company needs to include third-party Python libraries, such as NumPy and pandas, along with custom dependencies that are not part of the default SageMaker scikit-learn container. The goal is to keep operational overhead as low as possible while ensuring all dependencies are included for the training job.

Which solution will run the training job?

- Use the SageMaker pre-built scikit-learn container and install necessary third-party libraries, such as NumPy and pandas, at runtime in the training script using `pip install`.
- Create a custom Docker image with all necessary dependencies and push it to Amazon ECR.
- Use the default SageMaker scikit-learn container to run the Cox model training job without modifying the container.

    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/amazon-sagemaker-training-job-092424.png)
    
    If a pre-built SageMaker container does not meet all your requirements, you can extend the existing image to suit your needs. Even if there is direct support for your environment or framework, you might want to add extra functionalities or configure the container environment differently. By extending a pre-built image, you can take advantage of the included deep-learning libraries and settings without creating an image from scratch. You can modify the container to add libraries, adjust settings, and install additional dependencies.
