Push this new image to Amazon ECR and use it for the training job.** This solution ensures that all required dependencies, including third-party libraries and custom dependencies, are included in the Docker image.
    
    The option that says: **Create a custom Docker image with all necessary dependencies and push it to Amazon ECR** is incorrect because this solution involves creating a custom Docker image from scratch, which can be more complex and time-consuming compared to extending an existing SageMaker container. It doesn’t leverage the pre-built SageMaker container, which already includes many useful configurations and optimizations.
    
    The option that says: **Use the SageMaker pre-built scikit-learn container and install necessary third-party libraries, such as NumPy and pandas, at runtime in the training script using `pip install`** is incorrect because installing dependencies at runtime primarily increases the startup time of the training job and may lead to inconsistencies if the installation fails or if different versions of libraries are installed. This approach adds operational overhead and complexity, as you must manage the installation process within your training script.
    
    The option that says: **Use the default SageMaker scikit-learn container to run the Cox model training job without modifying the container** is incorrect. This option does not meet the requirement of including third-party libraries and custom dependencies that are not part of the default SageMaker scikit-learn container. The training job would typically fail due to missing dependencies.
    

An ML Engineer is working with a dataset containing customer purchasing behaviors for an e-commerce startup. The dataset includes attributes such as purchase amount, time spent on the website, and product categories, but it lacks labels or target values. The engineer aims to uncover hidden data patterns to better understand customer segments and enhance marketing strategies using Amazon SageMaker’s built-in algorithms.

Which unsupervised learning techniques would be most suitable for the analysis?

- Anomaly detection to find unusual purchasing behaviors that might indicate fraudulent activities.
- Density estimation to understand the distribution of purchase amounts across the customer base.
- **Cluster analysis to group customers with similar purchasing patterns for targeted marketing.**
    
    **Cluster analysis** is a powerful technique in unsupervised learning that helps identify natural groupings or segments within a dataset without predefined labels or target values. K-means is the commonly used clustering algorithm, which assigns data points to a fixed number of clusters. The algorithm iteratively refines the cluster centroids to minimize the variance within each group. This approach is widely used in various business applications, such as customer segmentation, market research, and behavioral analysis, to drive effective decision-making by understanding natural groupings.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/k-means-sagemaker-1_01OCT2023.gif)
    
    Amazon SageMaker includes built-in support for the K-Means clustering algorithm. This allows machine learning engineers to easily apply it to large-scale datasets in a fully managed and scalable environment. SageMaker simplifies the process of training, testing, and deploying clustering models by offering automated data processing, hyperparameter tuning, and integration with other AWS services like S3 and SageMaker Studio. This enables businesses to efficiently discover actionable insights, such as customer segments with similar purchasing patterns, without the complexity of managing infrastructure or developing algorithms from scratch. Cluster analysis, especially when executed on SageMaker, enhances marketing, recommendation systems, and product strategies by leveraging the underlying data patterns.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Cluster analysis to group customers with similar purchasing patterns for targeted marketing.**
    
    The option that says: **Dimension reduction to visualize high-dimensional customer data and identify the most relevant features** is incorrect. This option would simply reduce the complexity of data by projecting it into lower-dimensional spaces. While these techniques can help visualize or remove irrelevant features, they do not focus on grouping data points into meaningful clusters, which is essential for segmenting customers based on their purchasing behaviors.
    
    The option that says: **Anomaly detection to find unusual purchasing behaviors that might indicate fraudulent activities** is incorrect because this technique is only designed to flag data points that differ significantly from the majority. While useful in fraud detection and identifying outliers, anomaly detection is not intended to uncover the broad patterns or relationships that define customer segments. In this case, the objective is to group customers with similar behavior, which requires clustering rather than detecting anomalies.
    
    The option that says: **Density estimation to understand the distribution of purchase amounts across the customer base** is incorrect because it primarily focuses on modeling the distribution of a continuous variable, like purchase amounts, to determine its probability density function. Although it provides insights into the distribution of a single feature, it does not perform segmentation or clustering of customers based on multiple attributes.
    
- Dimension reduction to visualize high-dimensional customer data and identify the most relevant features.

An ML Engineer is creating a model to estimate the energy consumption of households based on factors such as square footage, number of occupants, and energy efficiency ratings. The Engineer trains a regression model using historical energy usage data. After the model is trained, the Engineer wants to visualize its performance to check if it tends to underestimate or overestimate energy consumption.

Which visualization technique should the engineer choose to evaluate the model’s performance?

- Receiver-operating characteristic curve (ROC)
- Confusion matrix
- Scatter plot
- **Residual plot**
    
    A **residual plot** is a visual tool often used in regression analysis to evaluate how well a predictive model performs by comparing the differences (residuals) between actual and predicted values. In machine learning, residuals represent the errors or discrepancies between the true data points and the values predicted by the model. A residual plot helps to identify underestimation or overestimation patterns by plotting residuals on the y-axis and either the predicted values or another relevant independent variable on the x-axis. Ideally, the residuals should be randomly scattered around the horizontal axis (indicating no clear pattern), which suggests that the model predictions are unbiased.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/histogram_01OCT2023.png)
    
    When analyzing a residual plot, if the points consistently lie above or below zero, it indicates that the model may be biased, underfitting, or overfitting the data. For example, if the residuals form a specific pattern, such as a curve, it could suggest that the model is missing important non-linear relationships. This makes the residual plot a crucial tool for evaluating the model, allowing engineers to adjust features, apply regularization, or choose a different model type to improve prediction accuracy.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Residual plot.**
    
    The option that says: **Confusion matrix** is incorrect because it is a tool used only to evaluate classification models, not regression models. It helps assess how well a model performs in classifying data into predefined categories (e.g., true positive, true negative, false positive, and false negative). In this scenario, the model predicts continuous values for energy consumption, not classes, so a confusion matrix would not apply.
    
    The option that says: **Receiver-operating characteristic curve (ROC)** is incorrect because it is primarily designed to evaluate binary or multi-class classification models by plotting the true positive rate against the false positive rate at various threshold levels. ROC curves are not suitable for regression models, which deal with predicting continuous outcomes like energy consumption and not classifying between categories.
    
    The option that says: **Scatter plot** is incorrect. While this option can be used to visualize the relationship between predicted and actual values, it does not directly show the residuals or errors in the predictions. Scatter plots can highlight general trends but do not provide insight into whether the model is systematically underestimating or overestimating values, which is better visualized using a residual plot.
    

An ML engineer is developing a natural language processing (NLP) model. The engineer must preprocess a large text dataset from multiple sources and create a scalable and automated pipeline that handles the machine learning workflow, from data ingestion to model deployment, while minimizing manual intervention.

Which combination of AWS services would be most suitable for this requirement? (Select TWO)

- Set up Amazon Kinesis Data Streams to ingest the text data from various sources.
- Use AWS Glue to extract, transform, and load the text data into Amazon S3.
- **Use Amazon SageMaker Pipelines to automate the entire ML workflow, including data preprocessing, model training, and deployment.**
- **Use Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler to clean and preprocess the data.**
- Use Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow (MWAA) to orchestrate data workflows and pipeline management.
- **Evaluation**
    
    **Natural Language Processing (NLP)** is a branch of artificial intelligence that deals with analyzing, understanding, and generating human language data. NLP models often require extensive preprocessing of text data, such as tokenization, stemming, and removing stop words.
    
    **SageMaker Data Wrangle**r is an excellent choice for preprocessing large text datasets. It provides a user-friendly interface and powerful data transformation capabilities, making it easier to clean and preprocess text data for NLP tasks. Data Wrangler can handle various data formats and supports common NLP preprocessing techniques like tokenization, stemming, and stop word removal.
    
    **SageMaker Pipelines** is the ideal service for automating the entire ML workflow, from data ingestion to model deployment. It allows you to define and run a pipeline with multiple steps, including data preprocessing (using Data Wrangler), model training, model evaluation, and model deployment. SageMaker Pipelines ensures consistent and reproducible workflows, minimizing manual intervention and reducing the risk of errors.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-sagemaker-pipeline-24-09-2024.png)
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Use Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler to clean and preprocess the data.**
    
    **– Use Amazon SageMaker Pipelines to automate the entire ML workflow, including data preprocessing, model training, and deployment.**
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Glue to extract, transform, and load the text data into Amazon S3** is incorrect. While AWS Glue is a powerful data integration service, it is primarily designed for Extract, Transform, and Load (ETL) operations on structured and semi-structured data. For NLP tasks involving text data, SageMaker Data Wrangler is a more specialized and suitable tool.
    
    The option that says: **Set up Amazon Kinesis Data Streams to ingest the text data from various sources** is incorrect. Kinesis Data Streams is a service primarily for ingesting and processing real-time streaming data. While it can be used for ingesting text data from various sources, it is not relevant for preprocessing and building ML pipelines. Data Wrangler and SageMaker Pipelines provide more comprehensive solutions for the given requirements.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow (MWAA) to orchestrate data workflows and pipeline management** is incorrect. Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow (MWAA) is a service typically for orchestrating and managing data pipelines and workflows. While it can be used for scheduling and coordinating ML workflows, SageMaker Pipelines is a more specialized and integrated solution for end-to-end ML pipelines within the AWS ecosystem.
    

A healthcare organization is developing a RAG-based chatbot to help patients find information on medical conditions, treatments, and services.The organization has a large collection of unstructured medical documents, research papers, and patient education materials in formats like PDFs, Word, and HTML. The chatbot will use generative AI and LLMs (Large Language models) to interact with users and provide accurate information based on their queries.

Which solutions should the organization consider to make the medical documents searchable and usable for the RAG-based chatbot? (Select TWO.)

- Store the documents in an Amazon S3 bucket and use Amazon Athena to query them for Retrieval Augmented Generation.
- Ingest the documents into an Amazon DynamoDB table for Retrieval Augmented Generation.
- **Index the documents using Amazon OpenSearch Service Vector Database for Retrieval Augmented Generation.**
- Upload the documents into Amazon RDS for structured queries using SQL.
- **Integrate the documents into Amazon Bedrock knowledge bases for Retrieval Augmented Generation.**
- **Evaluation**
    
    **Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG)** is a technique used in natural language processing (NLP) and generative AI systems, where a retrieval component (e.g., a search engine) is combined with a generative component (e.g., a language model) to produce more accurate and informative responses. The retrieval component searches for relevant information from a knowledge base or corpus, and the generative component uses this information to generate a coherent and contextual response.
    
    **Amazon OpenSearch Service** is a fully managed service that makes it easy to deploy, operate, and scale OpenSearch clusters. It supports vector search, which is a crucial component of Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) for chatbots. Vector search allows you to index and search unstructured data based on semantic similarity, making it suitable for RAG-based chatbots that need to retrieve relevant information from a large corpus of documents.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-opensearch-27-08-2024.png)
    
    **Amazon Bedrock** is a fully managed service that provides a knowledge base for storing and querying unstructured data. It supports Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) for chatbots and question-answering systems by indexing and searching unstructured data based on semantic similarity.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/amazon-bedrock-knowledge-bases-091624.png)
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Index the documents using Amazon OpenSearch Service Vector Database for Retrieval Augmented Generation.**
    
    **– Integrate the documents into Amazon Bedrock knowledge bases for Retrieval Augmented Generation.**
    
    The option that says: **Upload the documents into Amazon RDS for structured queries using SQL** is incorrect. Amazon Relational Database Service (RDS) is a managed service primarily for setting up and operating relational databases. It is designed for structured data and SQL queries, which are not suitable for unstructured data like PDFs, Word documents, and HTML files. RAG-based chatbots require indexing and searching unstructured data based on semantic similarity, which is not the primary use case for RDS.
    
    The option that says: **Store the documents in an Amazon S3 bucket and use Amazon Athena to query them for Retrieval Augmented Generation**is incorrect. Amazon Simple Storage Service (S3) is an object storage service, and while it can store unstructured data like documents, it does not provide built-in capabilities for indexing and searching the content of those documents based on semantic similarity. Amazon Athena is a query service for analyzing data in S3 using SQL, but it is primarily designed for structured and semi-structured data formats like CSV, JSON, and Parquet, not unstructured data like PDFs and Word documents.
    
    The option that says: **Ingest the documents into an Amazon DynamoDB table for Retrieval Augmented Generation** is incorrect. Amazon DynamoDB is a fully managed NoSQL database service that supports key-value and document data models. While it can store unstructured data as documents, it does not provide built-in capabilities for indexing and searching the content of those documents based on semantic similarity, which is a crucial requirement for RAG-based chatbots.
    

A healthcare organization is developing a RAG-based chatbot to help patients find information on medical conditions, treatments, and services.The organization has a large collection of unstructured medical documents, research papers, and patient education materials in formats like PDFs, Word, and HTML. The chatbot will use generative AI and LLMs (Large Language models) to interact with users and provide accurate information based on their queries.

How should the organization retrieve relevant documents to answer users’ queries effectively?

- Tag documents with metadata and create rule-based searches for document retrieval.
- Use the user's plaintext query to run a full-text search across the knowledge base and filter the results based on relevance scores.
- Use Amazon SageMaker's built-in algorithms to compare the user's query with the stored documents and select the top results.
- **Use the Amazon Titan Text Embeddings model to encode the user’s query and find the most similar documents based on a vector similarity search.**
    
    **Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG)** is a technique used in LLMs to improve the quality of generated responses by retrieving relevant information from a knowledge base or corpus of documents. The RAG model first retrieves relevant documents or passages based on the input query, and then uses the retrieved information to generate a more informed and accurate response.
    
    Document retrieval is the process of finding relevant documents or passages from a collection of documents based on a user’s query or search terms. This is a crucial step in the RAG-based chatbot system, as the quality of the retrieved documents directly impacts the accuracy and relevance of the generated responses.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-amazon-titan-text-embedding-25-09-2024.png)
    
    **Amazon Titan Text Embeddings** is a pre-trained language model that can be used to generate vector representations (embeddings) of text data. These embeddings can be used for various natural language processing tasks, including document retrieval. By encoding the user’s query and the documents into vectors, you can perform a vector similarity search to find the most relevant documents based on their semantic similarity to the query.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use the Amazon Titan Text Embeddings model to encode the user’s query and find the most similar documents based on a vector similarity search.**
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon SageMaker’s built-in algorithms to compare the user’s query with the stored documents and select the top results** is incorrect because Amazon SageMaker does not have built-in algorithms specifically designed for comparing user queries with documents and retrieving relevant results. SageMaker is a machine learning service that primarily provides tools and frameworks for building, training, and deploying machine learning models, but it does not have out-of-the-box functionality for document retrieval or information retrieval tasks.
    
    The option that says: **Use the user’s plaintext query to run a full-text search across the knowledge base and filter the results based on relevance scores** is incorrect. Running a full-text search across the knowledge base using the user’s plaintext query can be a valid approach for document retrieval. However, relying solely on relevance scores based on keyword matching may not be sufficient for complex queries or when dealing with large document collections. Incorporating semantic understanding and vector similarity techniques is preferred for deeper context matching between queries and documents.
    
    The option that says: **Tag documents with metadata and create rule-based searches for document retrieval** is incorrect because while tagging documents with metadata and creating rule-based searches can be typically useful for filtering, it doesn’t leverage the power of embeddings and vector search, which are more suitable for retrieving relevant unstructured data based on natural language queries.
    

A healthcare organization is developing a RAG-based chatbot to help patients find information on medical conditions, treatments, and services.The organization has a large collection of unstructured medical documents, research papers, and patient education materials in formats like PDFs, Word, and HTML. The chatbot will use generative AI and LLMs (Large Language models) to interact with users and provide accurate information based on their queries.

How should the organization provide an accurate and contextually relevant answer to the user’s query with the LEAST operational overhead?

- Use a pre-trained summarization model from Amazon SageMaker to condense the retrieved documents and provide a summarized response.
- Use Amazon SageMaker to fine-tune a question-answering model with the retrieved documents and return the model's output as the answer.
- Perform keyword matching between the user's query and the retrieved documents, returning the most relevant passage as the answer.
- **Engineer a prompt that includes the user's query and relevant document chunks, then send it to an LLM using Amazon Bedrock to generate a response.**
    
    **Prompt engineering** is a technique to optimize the input prompts given to a large language model (LLM) or foundation model (FM) to achieve desired outputs. This method is particularly effective for guiding the model’s behavior, tone, and style without needing extensive and costly adjustments, such as fine-tuning the model on new datasets. By carefully crafting prompts with appropriate context, keywords, and instructions, users can control how the model responds, ensuring that the output aligns with specific business needs, such as a company’s branding and conversational style.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/amazon-bedrock-knowledge-bases-091624.png)
    
    **Amazon Bedrock** is a fully managed service that simplifies building and scaling generative AI applications. It allows organizations to access pre-trained LLMs for text generation, summarization, and answering queries. By engineering a prompt that combines the user’s query with relevant document chunks and sending it to an LLM, the organization can utilize the advanced capabilities of the model to generate a well-contextualized response based on the documents.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Engineer a prompt that includes the user’s query and relevant document chunks, then send it to an LLM using Amazon Bedrock to generate a response.**
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon SageMaker to fine-tune a question-answering model with the retrieved documents and return the model’s output as the answer** is incorrect. Fine-tuning a model with the retrieved documents typically requires more operational overhead and resources, making it less efficient for immediate use. Using pre-trained LLMs from Amazon Bedrock would provide faster and more efficient results without retraining a model.
    
    The option that says: **Perform keyword matching between the user’s query and the retrieved documents, returning the most relevant passage as the answer** is incorrect because keyword matching lacks the depth of understanding typically provided by LLMs, potentially leading to inaccurate or incomplete answers.
    
    The option that says: **Use a pre-trained summarization model from Amazon SageMaker to condense the retrieved documents and provide a summarized response** is incorrect because it focuses mainly on summarizing the retrieved documents rather than directly answering the user’s query. While summarization can be useful in certain scenarios, it may not provide a direct and contextually relevant answer to the user’s specific query, especially if the query requires understanding and reasoning across multiple documents 
    

A healthcare organization is developing a RAG-based chatbot to help patients find information on medical conditions, treatments, and services.The organization has a large collection of unstructured medical documents, research papers, and patient education materials in formats like PDFs, Word, and HTML. The chatbot will use generative AI and LLMs (Large Language models) to interact with users and provide accurate information based on their queries.

How can the organization ensure the RAG-based chatbot continues improving and provides accurate, timely responses to user queries?

- Implement a rule-based system to curate and update the knowledge base with new information.
- **Implement a feedback loop where users can rate the chatbot's responses, and use this feedback to fine-tune the LLM and improve prompt engineering.**
    
    A Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) model combines the strengths of retrieval-based systems and generative models, such as large language models (LLMs). In a healthcare chatbot scenario, the system should provide accurate information and continuously improve based on user feedback and updated knowledge. This feedback loop is often referred to as
    
    **Reinforcement Learning with Human Feedback (RLHF)**
    
    , where human ratings or preferences are used to fine-tune the model’s performance. This approach helps the system align with user expectations and improve response quality over time.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-RLHF-28-08-2024.png)
    
    Implementing a feedback loop, commonly seen in RLHF, is essential for fine-tuning the chatbot’s responses over time. By allowing users to rate the chatbot’s answers, the organization can gather insights into the accuracy and relevance of the information provided. This feedback is then used to improve the performance of the LLM and refine prompt engineering, leading to more accurate and contextually relevant responses in future interactions.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Implement a feedback loop where users can rate the chatbot’s responses, and use this feedback to fine-tune the LLM and improve prompt engineering.**
    
    The option that says: **Periodically update the knowledge base with new medical documents and retrain the embedding model from scratch** is incorrect. While updating the knowledge base with new information is essential, retraining the model from scratch periodically may not be the most optimal approach. Doing this can be computationally expensive and may lead to catastrophic forgetting, where the model forgets previously learned information.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Comprehend to analyze the user’s queries and the chatbot’s responses to identify areas for improvement** is incorrect because Amazon Comprehend is primarily used for natural language processing tasks, such as sentiment analysis and entity extraction. While it can provide insights, it does not directly improve the performance of the RAG model or chatbot. This option alone is not sufficient to ensure the chatbot’s continuous improvement.
    
    The option that says: **Implement a rule-based system to curate and update the knowledge base with new information** is incorrect. While a rule-based system can help curate and update the knowledge base, it may not be the most effective approach for a generative AI system like an RAG-based chatbot. Rule-based systems are typically more suitable for narrow, well-defined domains and may struggle to handle the complexity and nuances of natural language interactions. A more effective approach would be to leverage machine learning techniques, such as fine-tuning the LLM and improving prompt engineering.
    
- Periodically update the knowledge base with new medical documents and retrain the embedding model from scratch.
- Use Amazon Comprehend to analyze the user's queries and the chatbot's responses to identify areas for improvement.

An ML Engineer manages machine learning models deployed on Amazon SageMaker. The engineer must audit actions such as model training, endpoint updates, and notebook access. The company requires a solution that can log and track these activities for compliance purposes, allowing for historical analysis of API calls and detection of any unusual activity. The audit logs should be securely stored and easily accessible for future investigations.

Which service can meet this requirement for monitoring and auditing the activities in Amazon SageMaker?

- Amazon S3
- Amazon SageMaker Debugger
- Amazon CloudWatch Logs
- **AWS CloudTrail**
    
    **AWS CloudTrail** is a service that allows you to audit and log API calls made in Amazon SageMaker, along with other AWS services. CloudTrail helps track actions such as model training, endpoint updates, and notebook access by logging the API calls made via the AWS Management Console, SDKs, and other AWS services. CloudTrail monitors API activity and keeps it in log files that can be used for compliance, security audits, and operational analysis. The logs are safely kept in Amazon S3 and can be retrieved for future investigations.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/AWS-Cloudtrail-25Sept2024.jpg)
    
    CloudTrail captures all API calls related to SageMaker activities, allowing for historical analysis of events. This feature enables users to detect unusual activity and perform a full audit of all API calls to ensure compliance and security.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **AWS CloudTrail.**
    
    The option that says: **Amazon CloudWatch Logs** is incorrect because this service is primarily used for monitoring and logging application-level events, such as logs from running applications or AWS services. It does not provide the auditing capabilities required for tracking API calls and compliance monitoring across multiple services.
    
    The option that says: **Amazon SageMaker Debugger** is incorrect because it is simply used to monitor and debug machine learning training jobs in real-time. While it helps detect issues during training, it is not designed for auditing API activities or compliance monitoring.
    
    The option that says: **Amazon S3** is incorrect because Amazon S3 is just a storage service. While audit logs can be stored in S3, the service does not provide the tracking and monitoring functionality required for auditing API calls and actions in Amazon SageMaker.
    

A data scientist is training a machine learning model using Amazon SageMaker. The training data is currently stored in an on-premises SQL Server database. There is dedicated network connectivity between the on-premises environment and AWS. The data scientist intends to use Amazon SageMaker’s pre-built Docker images for training.

How should the data from the on-premises SQL Server be ingested into Amazon SageMaker for model training?

- Use AWS DataSync to transfer the data directly from the on-premises SQL Server to Amazon SageMaker.
- **Use AWS Database Migration Service (DMS) to migrate the data from the on-premises SQL Server to an Amazon S3 bucket, and then configure SageMaker to access the data from S3.**
    
    AWS Database Migration Service (AWS DMS) is a cloud-based service that facilitates the migration of relational databases, data warehouses, NoSQL databases, and other types of data stores. It allows you to migrate your data to the AWS Cloud or between various cloud and on-premises configurations.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/aws-dms-092524.png)
    
    When setting up a training job in Amazon SageMaker, you need to specify the location of your training dataset and choose an input mode for accessing it. Amazon SageMaker supports data storage in Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3), Amazon Elastic File System (Amazon EFS), and Amazon FSx for Lustre. The input modes determine whether the dataset is streamed in real-time or downloaded entirely at the start of the training job.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/sagemaker-training-dataset-data-location-092524.png)
    
    AWS DMS is designed to migrate data from different databases to AWS services, including Amazon S3. Once the data is in S3, Amazon SageMaker can easily access it for training purposes. This approach is efficient and takes advantage of S3’s scalability and flexibility for storing large datasets. It also follows best practices by separating storage and computing resources.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use AWS Database Migration Service (DMS) to migrate the data from the on-premises SQL Server to an Amazon S3 bucket, and then configure SageMaker to access the data from S3.**
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS DataSync to transfer the data directly from the on-premises SQL Server to Amazon SageMaker** is incorrect because AWS DataSync is primarily used for transferring files between on-premises storage and AWS storage services like S3, EFS, or FSx. It is not designed to handle direct database migrations or to interface directly with Amazon SageMaker.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Glue to extract the data from the on-premises SQL Server and load it directly into Amazon SageMaker** is incorrect because AWS Glue is simply an ETL (Extract, Transform, Load) service for extracting data from various sources, transform it, and load it into data stores like S3 or Redshift. However, it does not load data directly into Amazon SageMaker. The typical workflow would involve using Glue to load data into S3, from where SageMaker can access it. This makes it an indirect and less straightforward approach compared to using DMS.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Database Migration Service (DMS) to migrate the data from the on-premises SQL Server to Amazon DynamoDB, and then configure SageMaker to access the data from DynamoDB** is incorrect. While AWS DMS can migrate data to Amazon DynamoDB, Amazon SageMaker does not support DynamoDB as a direct data source for training jobs. Additionally, DynamoDB is a NoSQL database, so migrating from SQL to DynamoDB would require restructuring and further transformations to prepare the data for model training, making this approach more complex.
    
- Use AWS Database Migration Service (DMS) to migrate the data from the on-premises SQL Server to Amazon DynamoDB, and then configure SageMaker to access the data from DynamoDB.
- Use AWS Glue to extract the data from the on-premises SQL Server and load it directly into Amazon SageMaker.

A data scientist is using Amazon SageMaker’s k-Nearest Neighbors (k-NN) algorithm to build a machine learning model. The initial dataset has 2,000 data points, and the <code>sample_size</code> is set to 200. The model is configured to run on a multi-worker setup with 2 instances. Each worker is tasked with sampling an equal number of points from the dataset.

Which of the following statements is correct about how the data points will be handled in this scenario?

- Each worker will sample 1,000 points from the dataset.
- Each worker will sample 2,000 points from the dataset.
- **Each worker will sample 100 points from the dataset.**
    
    The **Amazon SageMaker k-nearest neighbors (k-NN) algorithm** works by looking at nearby data points to make predictions. It’s a simple method that doesn’t assume anything about the underlying data pattern (non-parametric).
    
    – For classification, such as predicting categories, the algorithm finds the “k” closest data points to the one being predicted and then looks at their labels (categories). It predicts the label that appears most often among those neighbors.
    
    – For regression, like predicting a value, like a number, it again finds the “k” nearest data points, but instead of picking a label, it calculates the average of their values and uses that as the predicted result.
    
    In both cases, the algorithm relies on how close points are to each other to make predictions based on existing data.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/amazon-sagemaker-k-NN-algorithms-3Oct2024.png)
    
    Amazon SageMaker’s k-NN algorithm is widely used for classification and regression problems. In this scenario, the k-NN algorithm is set to use a `sample_size` of 200, meaning that the model will sample 200 data points from the entire dataset, regardless of the number of workers. Since there are 2 workers in this scenario, each worker will sample an equal number of data points, which would result in 100 data points per worker (200 / 2 = 100).
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Each worker will sample 100 points from the dataset.**
    
    The option that says: **Each worker will sample 200 points from the dataset** is incorrect because the total `sample_size` parameter in the k-NN algorithm is set to 200, which is the total number of points sampled across all workers. Since there are 2 workers, each will only handle 100 points (200 / 2 = 100).
    
    The option that says: **Each worker will sample 1,000 points from the dataset** is incorrect because the total dataset contains 2,000 points, but the `sample_size` is set to 200. The workers only handle a portion of this smaller sample size, not 1,000 points each.
    
    The option that says: **Each worker will sample 2,000 points from the dataset** is incorrect because the entire dataset is not being sampled. The `sample_size` parameter limits the number of points being processed to 200, not the full 2,000 data points.
    
- Each worker will sample 200 points from the dataset.

An ML engineer is using Amazon SageMaker to train a convolutional neural network (CNN) model for an image classification project. The engineer wants to ensure they follow best practices for setting up the training job and optimizing the model’s performance.

Which of the following must be taken to correctly set up and train the model on Amazon SageMaker?

- **Utilize Amazon SageMaker’s built-in support for TensorFlow or PyTorch to define and train the CNN model on SageMaker directly.**
    
    Amazon SageMaker offers a variety of built-in algorithms, pre-trained models, and pre-built solution templates to help data scientists and machine learning practitioners quickly start training and deploying machine learning models.
    
    The Amazon SageMaker Image Classification – TensorFlow algorithm is a supervised learning algorithm that supports transfer learning with numerous pre-trained models from TensorFlow Hub. You can use transfer learning to fine-tune one of these pre-trained models on your own dataset, even if you don’t have a large amount of image data. This algorithm inputs an image and outputs a probability for each provided class label. Training datasets must consist of images in .jpg, .jpeg, or .png format.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/sagemaker-image-classification-092624.png)
    
    You can also use Amazon SageMaker to train and deploy a model using custom PyTorch code. The SageMaker Python SDK PyTorch estimators and models, along with the SageMaker open-source PyTorch container, simplify the process of writing a PyTorch script and running it in SageMaker.
    
    Amazon SageMaker supports popular deep learning frameworks like TensorFlow and PyTorch, which are ideal for defining and training convolutional neural networks (CNNs) for image classification tasks. By leveraging SageMaker’s built-in support, ML engineers can take advantage of SageMaker’s managed infrastructure, distributed training capabilities, and hyperparameter tuning to optimize model performance.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Utilize Amazon SageMaker’s built-in support for TensorFlow or PyTorch to define and train the CNN model on SageMaker directly.**
    
    The option that says: **Train the CNN model on a local machine and then upload the trained model to SageMaker for deployment** is incorrect. While it is possible to train a model locally and then upload it to SageMaker for deployment, this approach does not take full advantage of SageMaker’s capabilities. Training directly on SageMaker allows the engineer to utilize its scalable infrastructure and integrated tools for monitoring and optimizing the training process.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon SageMaker’s built-in XGBoost algorithm to train the CNN model** is incorrect because XGBoost is a powerful algorithm typically for gradient boosting on decision trees, but it is not suitable for training convolutional neural networks (CNNs), which are specifically designed for tasks like image classification. CNNs require deep learning frameworks like TensorFlow or PyTorch.
    
    The option that says: **Use the built-in Linear Learner algorithm for image classification tasks** is incorrect because the Linear Learner algorithm is primarily designed for linear models and is not suitable for complex tasks like image classification that require deep learning models such as CNNs. For image classification, deep learning frameworks like TensorFlow or PyTorch are more appropriate.
    
- Use the built-in Linear Learner algorithm for image classification tasks.
- Train the CNN model on a local machine and then upload the trained model to SageMaker for deployment.
- Use Amazon SageMaker’s built-in XGBoost algorithm to train the CNN model.

An ML Engineer is developing a model to predict product demand based on historical sales data, seasonal trends, and other relevant factors. The engineer needs to automate the weekly execution of the prediction model to update the company’s inventory and supply chain management systems. The company has decided to set up an Amazon SageMaker Pipelines workflow to achieve this automation.

Choose and arrange the steps from the list below the ML Engineer should follow to automate the weekly execution of the prediction model. (Select and order THREE.)

- Create a SageMaker Pipelines pipeline using the trained model to include steps for batch inference.
- Create a SageMaker Pipelines pipeline using the trained model to include steps for asynchronous inference.
- Create an IAM role for Amazon EventBridge with the `SageMaker::StartPipelineExecution`permission.
- Create an IAM role for Amazon EventBridge with the `SageMaker::SendPipelineExecutionStepSuccess`permission.
- Create a new event bus in Amazon EventBridge to manage events related to SageMaker pipeline execution.
- Set up a rule in Amazon EventBridge to trigger the pipeline execution at the beginning of every week.
- **Answer**
    
    Step 1 - Create a SageMaker Pipelines pipeline using the trained model to include steps for batch inference.
    
    Step 2 - Create an IAM role for Amazon EventBridge with the `SageMaker::StartPipelineExecution`permission.
    
    Step 3 - Set up a rule in Amazon EventBridge to trigger the pipeline execution at the beginning of every week.
    
    **Amazon SageMaker Pipelines** is a service that allows you to create, automate, and manage end-to-end ML workflows. It provides a consistent and reproducible way to build, train, and deploy ML models at scale.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-sagemaker-pipeline-24-09-2024.png)
    
    **Batch inference** is the process of using a trained machine learning model to make predictions on a large dataset in a batch mode. In batch inference, the entire dataset is processed at once, and the predictions are generated for all the data points in the dataset. This is typically used when you need to generate predictions for a large amount of data, and real-time inference is not required.
    
    SageMaker Pipelines is designed to automate the end-to-end machine learning workflow, including **batch inference**. The ML Engineer needs to create a pipeline that includes a step for batch inference to generate predictions based on the trained model and input data.
    
    **Amazon EventBridge** is a serverless event bus service provided by AWS. It enables you to connect applications with data sources and other services using events. EventBridge acts as a central hub for event ingestion and delivery, allowing you to build event-driven architectures and automate workflows.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-eventbrdige-rules-weekly-27-09-24.png)
    
    Amazon EventBridge can trigger the SageMaker Pipelines execution based on a recurring schedule or specific event. Setting up a rule in EventBridge to trigger the pipeline execution at the beginning of every week will automate the weekly execution of the prediction model. To allow Amazon EventBridge to trigger the SageMaker Pipelines execution, an IAM role with the `SageMaker::StartPipelineExecution permission` is required. This permission grants the ability to start a pipeline execution.
    
    **Step 1: Create a SageMaker Pipelines pipeline using the trained model to include steps for batch inference.**
    
    **Step 2: Create an IAM role for Amazon EventBridge with the `SageMaker::StartPipelineExecution`permission.**
    
    **Step 3: Set up a rule in Amazon EventBridge to trigger the pipeline execution at the beginning of every week.**
    
    The option that says: **Create a SageMaker Pipelines pipeline using the trained model to include steps for asynchronous inference** is incorrect because Asynchronous inference is typically used for real-time inference, where predictions are generated for individual data points as they arrive. This scenario involves batch processing, which is more suitable for periodic, scheduled tasks like demand forecasting.
    
    The option that says: **Create an IAM role for Amazon EventBridge with the `SageMaker::SendPipelineExecutionStepSuccess`permission** is incorrect. The `SageMaker::SendPipelineExecutionStepSuccess`permission is primarily used by SageMaker Pipelines to send status updates about the pipeline execution steps and is not used to trigger the pipeline execution.
    
    The option that says: **Create a new event bus in Amazon EventBridge to manage events related to SageMaker pipeline execution** is incorrect because an additional event bus is not required for setting up a scheduled trigger for SageMaker Pipelines. EventBridge’s existing functionality can handle this through rules.
